A Verbose Note on Density Functional Theory
Chang Liu

Abstract
This note is intended for expanding the details on the derivation and properties of density functional theory, in hope to make them more systematic, better motivated, and step-by-step for readers new to the domain. The note starts with basic concepts in quantum mechanics, then takes the step towards many-body systems using the tools of second quantization and Fock space, with some highlights on properties of the Coulomb system. Given these general technical preparations, the Hartree-Fock method is naturally unrolled, with expressions for various cases and quantities. Density functional theory is then presented, with a motivating reasoning of the Kohn-Sham formulation, and a connection and comparison of the expressions with the Hartree-Fock method. Lieb’s celebrated 1983 paper on functional analysis on density functionals is also summarized.



0
Preamble

0.1
Notation

•

Let
\vec{r}=(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
denote a general spacial position.
For multiple particles (electrons), let
\vec{r}_{i}
denote the position of the
i
-th, and denote
\vec{\bm{r}}:=(\vec{r}_{1},\cdots,\vec{r}_{N})
.
The same goes for
\vec{p}
,
\nabla
, and spin
s
(except that it takes discrete values).

A molecular conformation is defined by the charges and positions of the
N_{\textnormal{A}}
nuclei
\{(Z_{A},\vec{r}_{A})\}_{A\in[N_{\textnormal{A}}]}
.

Let
\mathrm{x}:=(\vec{r},s)
, and similarly denote
\mathbf{x}:=(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
.
Define
\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,f(\mathrm{x}):=\sum_{s}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,f(\vec{r},s)
.

•

Upper (resp. lower) Greek letters
\Psi
,
\Phi
denote time-dependent (resp. time-independent) wavefunctions.

\psi
denotes a general wavefunction or a many-body wavefunction.
Others like
\phi,\eta,\chi
denote one-body wavefunctions.

\psi_{(n)}
denotes an eigenvector of the
n
-th energy eigenstate with eigenvalue
E_{(n)}
.

\phi_{i}
denotes the
i
-th one-body wavefunction (orbital, basis, …).

\psi^{s}
or
\phi^{s}
is used for emphasizing the spacial wavefunction of a given spin
s
.
For specific value, use
\psi^{s=\frac{1}{2}}
or
\psi^{\uparrow}
.
Other cases denote the usual exponent.

\psi_{\textnormal{S}}
denotes a Slater-determinant wavefunction (often with orthonormal orbitals).

•

\rho
/
\Gamma
represents a general density / density matrix, and
\rho^{(1)}
,
\rho^{(2)}
/
P^{(1)}
,
P^{(2)}
represent reduced densities / density matrices.

\mathbf{\Gamma}
,
\mathbf{P}
are their matrix representations under a basis.

•

Function symbols with tilde (
e.g.
,
\tilde{\psi}
) represent the Fourier-transformed functions of the respective functions.

•

\hat{H}
,
\hat{p}
,
\hat{T}
,
\hat{V}
,
\hat{W}
denote physical operators on wavefunctions (Hermite/self-adjoint operators on Hilbert space).

\hat{V}
and
\hat{W}
are devoted for one-particle and two-particle operators, respectively.
The same symbols denote the respective extensions to apply on a many-body wavefunction, and in that case
\hat{V}_{i}
or
\hat{W}_{ij}
are used for the one-particle or two-particle operator on the specified particle(s)
(
\hat{V}_{\cdot}
or
\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}
for general one-particle or two-particle operator without specifying the operand).

\mathbf{V}
,
\mathbf{W}
are the corresponding matrix representations under a basis.
Superscript denotes matrix row index.
Sans-serif bolds,
e.g.
,
\bm{\mathsf{J}}
,
\bm{\mathsf{K}}
, denote tensor representations.

•

E_{\textnormal{subs}}^{\textnormal{supers}}
,
T_{\textnormal{subs}}^{\textnormal{supers}}
,
V_{\textnormal{subs}}^{\textnormal{supers}}
:
for the corresponding value or functional of wavefunction/density/density-matrix.
“
subs
” specifies what energy/potential it is (
e.g.
,
E_{\textnormal{ee}}
,
E_{\textnormal{ext}}
; for
T
, only
T_{\textnormal{S}}
is available, which denotes the kinetic energy of a non-interacting system;
V_{\textnormal{S}}
represents the effective potential for a non-interacting system), and “
supers
” specifies the version (often name initials).

•

F[f]
represents a functional that maps
f
to
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
,
while
g_{[f]}
represents a function (of
\vec{r}
,
\vec{\bm{r}}
,
\mathbf{x}
,
etc.
) or a functional determined by function
f
.

•

For
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
[N]:=\{1,\cdots,N\}
.
\sum_{i,i^{\prime}\in[N]}\cdots:=\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in[N]}\cdots
.
\sum_{i\neq j}\cdots:=\sum_{i}\sum_{j:\neq i}\cdots
.
\sum_{i<j}\cdots:=\sum_{i}\sum_{j:>i}\cdots
.
\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\cdots:=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\cdots
.
\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{i:j}\cdots:=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i+1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{j}\cdots
.

0.2
Functions of Complex Variable

A complex function
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C},z=(x+\mathrm{i}\,y)\mapsto u(x,y)+\mathrm{i}\,v(x,y)
is
complex-differentiable (analytic)
,
iff.

u
and
v
are real-differentiable and satisfy
Cauchy-Riemann equations
:

\displaystyle\partial_{x}u=\partial_{y}v,\quad\partial_{y}u=-\partial_{x}v.

(0.1)

To see the necessity,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{f(x_{0}+\mathrm{i}\,y_{0}+\varepsilon)-f_{0}}{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\lparen\frac{u(x_{0}+\varepsilon,y_{0})-u_{0}}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{i}\,\frac{v(x_{0}+\varepsilon,y_{0})-v_{0}}{\varepsilon}\right\rparen=\partial_{x}u+\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{x}v

and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{f(x_{0}+\mathrm{i}\,y_{0}+\mathrm{i}\,\varepsilon)-f_{0}}{\mathrm{i}\,\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\lparen\frac{u(x_{0},y_{0}+\varepsilon)-u_{0}}{\mathrm{i}\,\varepsilon}+\mathrm{i}\,\frac{v(x_{0},y_{0}+\varepsilon)-v_{0}}{\mathrm{i}\,\varepsilon}\right\rparen=-\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{y}u+\partial_{y}v
should coincide: both are
\partial_{z}f(z=x_{0}+\mathrm{i}\,y_{0})
.
Note
none
of
z/\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2}
,
z^{2}/\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2}
,
\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2}z
and even
\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2}
, is analytic.

In this note we use a more general notion of derivative of
f
:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}f(z=x+\mathrm{i}\,y):=\partial_{x}u(x,y)-\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{y}u(x,y),\qquad u(x,y):=\Re f(x+\mathrm{i}\,y).

(0.2)

When
f
is analytic, Eq. (
0.1
) gives
-\partial_{y}u=\partial_{x}v
so this
\partial_{z}f(z=x+\mathrm{i}\,y)=\partial_{x}u+\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{x}v
coincides with the standard derivative.
Even if
f(z)
is not analytic, it can be defined (as long as each
\partial_{x}u
and
\partial_{y}u
can),
and could already serve for variation calculation if
f=u
is itself real, since each of
x
and
y
is an individual/independent variable, and
\partial_{x}f=\partial_{y}f=0

iff.

\partial_{x}u-\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{y}u=0
.
If the function form is the same for both
x
and
y
,
i.e.
,
f(z)=f(x)+f(y)
(
e.g.
, the case for deriving Eq. (
1.29
)), then
\partial_{z^{*}}f
has the same form as treating
z
as real.

Under this notion,
\partial_{z_{1}^{*}}z_{1}^{*}z_{2}=z_{2}
,
\partial_{z_{1}}z_{1}^{*}z_{2}=z_{2}^{*}
hence also
\partial_{z_{1}^{*}}z_{1}z_{2}=z_{2}^{*}
, and
\partial_{z^{*}}\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2}=2z
,
\partial_{z}\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2}=2z^{*}
.

0.3
Gradient w.r.t Complex Tensors

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}f)^{i}:=\partial_{(\mathbf{v}^{\dagger})_{i}}f=\partial_{(\mathbf{v}^{i})^{*}}f,\quad\text{and similarly}\quad(\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}f)^{i}_{j}:=\partial_{(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})^{j}_{i}}f=\partial_{(\mathbf{A}^{i}_{j})^{*}}f.

(0.3)

When adapting the above generalized complex derivative Eq. (
0.2
),

\displaystyle\partial_{(\mathbf{v}^{i})^{*}}(\mathbf{v}^{j})^{*}z^{k}_{l}={}

\displaystyle(\partial_{\Re\mathbf{v}^{i}}+\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{\Im\mathbf{v}^{i}})(\Re\mathbf{v}^{j}\Re z^{k}_{l}+\Im\mathbf{v}^{j}\Im z^{k}_{l})=\delta^{i}_{j}\Re z^{k}_{l}+\mathrm{i}\,\delta^{i}_{j}\Im z^{k}_{l}=\delta^{i}_{j}z^{k}_{l},

(0.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{(\mathbf{v}^{i})^{*}}\mathbf{v}^{j}z^{k}_{l}={}

\displaystyle(\partial_{\Re\mathbf{v}^{i}}+\mathrm{i}\,\partial_{\Im\mathbf{v}^{i}})(\Re\mathbf{v}^{j}\Re z^{k}_{l}-\Im\mathbf{v}^{j}\Im z^{k}_{l})=\delta^{ij}\Re z^{k}_{l}-\mathrm{i}\,\delta^{ij}\Im z^{k}_{l}=\delta^{ij}(z^{k}_{l})^{*}=\delta^{ij}(z^{*})^{l^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\delta^{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{ll^{\prime}}.

(0.5)

Hence,

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}\mathbf{u})^{k}=\partial_{(\mathbf{v}^{k})^{*}}(\mathbf{v}^{i})^{*}\mathbf{u}^{i}=\delta^{k}_{i}\mathbf{u}^{i}=\mathbf{u}^{k}

\displaystyle\,\Longrightarrow\,\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u},

(0.6)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}\mathbf{u})^{k}=\partial_{(\mathbf{u}^{k})^{*}}(\mathbf{v}^{i})^{*}\mathbf{u}^{i}=\delta^{ki}\mathbf{v}^{i^{\prime}}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}=\delta^{k}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}^{i^{\prime}}=\mathbf{v}^{k}

\displaystyle\,\Longrightarrow\,\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v},

(0.7)

and

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v})^{k}=\partial_{(\mathbf{v}^{k})^{*}}(\mathbf{v}^{i})^{*}\mathbf{A}^{i}_{j}\mathbf{v}^{j}=\delta^{k}_{i}\mathbf{A}^{i}_{j}\mathbf{v}^{j}+\delta^{kj}\mathbf{v}^{i}(\mathbf{A}^{i}_{j^{\prime}})^{*}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}=\mathbf{A}^{k}_{j}\mathbf{v}^{j}+\delta^{k}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}^{i}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})^{j^{\prime}}_{i}=\mathbf{A}^{k}_{j}\mathbf{v}^{j}+\mathbf{v}^{i}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})^{k}_{i}

(0.8)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{}\,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})\mathbf{v}\,\mathrel{{\mathop{\Longrightarrow}\limits}}\,\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}=2\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}.

(0.9)

Also note
\nabla_{\mathbf{v}^{\dagger}}f=(\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}f)^{\dagger}
.

1.1
Origin and Interpretation of Schrödinger Equation

1.1.1
History

•

Originates from Planck’s explanation to black-body radiation.

•

Bohr explored the idea of quantization and successfully explained the electron distribution of hydrogen atom.

•

The foundation/principle of quantization is then developed in two ways: matrix mechanics (Heisenberg, Born, Pauli) and wave mechanics (Schrödinger).
They are found equivalent (Dirac) and are two pictures of quantum mechanics.

•

The Schrödinger picture is commonly considered.

1.1.2
Wavefunction: quantum description of physical state

De Broglie wave (1924): generalize the wave-particle duality from photons to matter particles.

•

A free particle with
(E,\vec{p})

\Longleftrightarrow
a plane wave

\displaystyle\Psi(\vec{r},t)=A\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,(\omega t-\vec{k}\cdot\vec{r}))=A\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,(Et-\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r})/\hbar),\quad\text{where }E=h\nu=\hbar\omega,\;\vec{p}=\text{`` }h/\vec{\lambda}\text{ ''}=\hbar\vec{k}.

(1.1)

•

A general particle
\Longleftrightarrow
Superposition of multiple plane waves:

\displaystyle\Psi(\vec{r},t)=(2\pi\hbar)^{-3/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\,\Phi(\vec{p},t)\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar),

(1.2)

where
\Phi(\vec{p},t)
is the “superposition coefficient” of different plane waves (different
\vec{p}
) from Fourier transform:
1
1

1

Why it holds over the unbounded space (while commonly it is over a period):
Let
\int{R}\mathrm{d}x\,\exp(\mathrm{i}\,kx)
denote the limit in a symmetric way:

\lim_{B\to\infty}\int_{-B}^{B}\mathrm{d}x\,\exp(\mathrm{i}\,kx)=\lim_{B\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}(\sin kx-\mathrm{i}\,\cos kx)|_{-B}^{B}=2\lim_{B\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\sin kB=:2g(k)
,
which is
2\pi\delta(k)
in the sense that:

\int{R}\mathrm{d}k\,f(k)g(k)=\int{R}\mathrm{d}k\,\lim_{B\to\infty}f(k)\frac{\sin kB}{k}=\int{R}\mathrm{d}(kB)\,\lim_{B\to\infty}f(kB/B)\frac{\sin kB}{kB}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\lim_{B\to\infty}\int{R}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,f(\alpha/B)\frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\int{R}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,f(0)\frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}=\pi f(0)
.
For the last equality,
\int{R}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\frac{\sin\alpha}{\alpha}\exp(-\nu\alpha)\big|_{\nu=0}=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\sin\alpha\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}v\,\exp(-v\alpha)=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}v\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\sin\alpha\exp(-v\alpha)=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}v\,\frac{1}{1+v^{2}}=2\arctan v\,|_{0}^{\infty}=\pi
, where we have used the Laplace transform of
\sin\alpha
.
This leads to:

\displaystyle\int{R}\mathrm{d}x\,\exp(\mathrm{i}\,kx)=2\pi\delta(k),\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\exp(\mathrm{i}\,(\vec{p}-\vec{p}^{\prime})\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar)=(2\pi\hbar)^{3}\delta(\vec{p}-\vec{p}^{\prime}).

(1.3)

\displaystyle\Phi(\vec{p},t)=(2\pi\hbar)^{-3/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\Psi(\vec{r},t)\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar).

(1.4)

•

Supported by the electron interference experiment (Davisson & Germer, 1927).

Born’s probabilistic interpretation of wavefunction (1926):

•

Particle density
\rho(\vec{r},t)=\left\lvert\Psi(\vec{r},t)\right\rvert^{2}
.

•

Mean position
\bar{\vec{r}}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})\vec{r}\psi(\vec{r})
at some time
t_{0}
(
\psi(\vec{r}):=\Psi(\vec{r},t_{0})
).

•

Mean momentum
\bar{\vec{p}}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\,\phi^{*}(\vec{p})\vec{p}\phi(\vec{p})
.
Using Fourier transform for
\phi
from
\psi
, we have:
2
2

2

\displaystyle\bar{\vec{p}}={}

\displaystyle(2\pi\hbar)^{-3/2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\Big(\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi(\vec{r})\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar)\Big)^{*}\vec{p}\phi(\vec{p})=(2\pi\hbar)^{-3/2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar)\vec{p}\phi(\vec{p})

(1.5)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(2\pi\hbar)^{-3/2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})\int\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\,\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar)\vec{p}\phi(\vec{p})=(2\pi\hbar)^{-3/2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})\int\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\,(-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar)\nabla\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar)\phi(\vec{p})

(1.6)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(2\pi\hbar)^{-3/2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})(-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar)\nabla\int\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\,\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r}/\hbar)\phi(\vec{p})=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})(-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar)\nabla\psi(\vec{r}).

(1.7)

\displaystyle\bar{\vec{p}}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})\hat{\vec{p}}\psi(\vec{r}),\quad\text{where $\hat{\vec{p}}:=-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\nabla$ is the momentum operator}.

(1.8)

•

Mean kinetic energy
\bar{T}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\,\phi^{*}(\vec{p})\frac{\vec{p}^{2}}{2m}\phi(\vec{p})
, or in terms of
\psi(\vec{r})
,

\bar{T}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi^{*}(\vec{r})\hat{T}\psi(\vec{r})
, where
\hat{T}:=\frac{\hat{\vec{p}}^{2}}{2m}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}
.

Quantum mechanics probability vs. statistical mechanics probability:

•

In QM, the distribution
\rho_{(n)}(\vec{r})=\left\lvert\psi_{(n)}(\vec{r})\right\rvert^{2}
describes the
spatial uncertainty in one state
. It is one state.

•

In SM, the distribution is over
states
:
p_{(n)}
for discrete energy states or
p(E)
for continuous energy states.

1.1.3
Schrödinger equation: quantum description of physical dynamics

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi(\vec{r},t)=\hat{H}\Psi(\vec{r},t),\quad\text{where $\hat{H}:=\hat{T}+V(\vec{r})$ is the Hamiltonian operator}.

(1.9)

Intuition 1:

For a free particle described by a plane wave function
\Psi(\vec{r},t)=A\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,(Et-\vec{p}\cdot\vec{r})/\hbar)
,
l.h.s
=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}\Psi(\vec{r},t)=\frac{\vec{p}^{2}}{2m}\Psi(\vec{r},t)=E\Psi(\vec{r},t)=
r.h.s.
For a general particle, since the equation is linear, it is similar by superpositioning plane waves.

Intuition 2:

Conservation of particle number/probability (when the potential
V
is real).
By Schrödinger equation,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho(\vec{r},t)}{\partial t}\equiv{}

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial\Psi^{*}}{\partial t}\Psi=\mathrm{i}\,\frac{\hbar}{2m}(\Psi^{*}\nabla^{2}\Psi-\Psi\nabla^{2}\Psi^{*})=-\nabla\cdot\vec{J},

(1.10)

\displaystyle\text{where }\vec{J}:={}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\,\frac{\hbar}{2m}(\Psi^{*}\nabla\Psi-\Psi\nabla\Psi^{*})=\frac{1}{2m}(\Psi^{*}\hat{\vec{p}}\Psi-\Psi\hat{\vec{p}}\Psi^{*})=\frac{1}{2m}(\Psi^{*}\hat{\vec{p}}\Psi+\Psi\hat{\vec{p}}^{*}\Psi^{*})

(1.11)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Re(\Psi^{*}\hat{\vec{v}}\Psi),\quad\hat{\vec{v}}:=\hat{\vec{p}}/m,

(1.12)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\rho(\vec{r},t)\frac{1}{2}\Big\lparen\frac{\hat{\vec{v}}\Psi}{\Psi}+\frac{\hat{\vec{v}}^{*}\Psi^{*}}{\Psi^{*}}\Big\rparen=\rho(\vec{r},t)\Re\big\lparen\frac{\hat{\vec{v}}\Psi}{\Psi}\big\rparen.

(1.13)

This is in the form of
continuity equation
.
Particularly, for a free particle,
\vec{J}=\frac{1}{2m}(\Psi^{*}\vec{p}\Psi+\Psi\vec{p}\Psi^{*})=\Psi^{*}\Psi\frac{\vec{p}}{m}=\rho\vec{v}
.

Intuition 3:

Schrödinger’s explanation to quantization as the discrete spectrum of
\hat{H}
, based on an analytical mechanics formulation. See Sec.
1.1.4
below.

Stationary Schrödinger equation

When
\hat{H}
does not explicitly depend on time,
\Psi(\vec{r},t)=\psi(\vec{r})\chi(t)
can be separated (not necessarily),
which turns the equation to
\psi(\vec{r})\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\chi^{\prime}(t)=(\hat{H}\psi(\vec{r}))\chi(t)
, or
\frac{1}{\chi(t)}\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\chi^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{\psi(\vec{r})}\hat{H}\psi(\vec{r})
.
The l.h.s is constant of
\vec{r}
and the r.h.s is constant of
t
, so both sides must be a constant
E
of both
\vec{r}
and
t
.
This leads to the original/stationary Schrödinger equation:

\displaystyle\hat{H}\psi(\vec{r})=E\psi(\vec{r}),

(1.14)

and
\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\chi^{\prime}(t)=E\chi(t)
whose solution is
\chi(t)=A\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,Et/\hbar)
.
A solution
(E_{(n)},\psi_{(n)})
to the stationary equation is called a
stationary state
or
eigenstate
.
Its total wavefunction

\displaystyle\Psi_{(n)}(\vec{r},t)=A_{(n)}\psi_{(n)}(\vec{r})\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,E_{(n)}t/\hbar)

(1.15)

makes mechanical quantities stationary (time-independent):

\bar{O}:=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\Psi_{(n)}^{*}(\vec{r},t)\hat{O}\Psi_{(n)}(\vec{r},t)=\left\lvert A_{(n)}\right\rvert^{2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi_{(n)}^{*}(\vec{r})\hat{O}\psi_{(n)}(\vec{r})
.

General solution in the time-independent Hamiltonian case is in the form

\displaystyle\Psi(\vec{r},t)=\sum_{n}c_{(n)}\psi_{(n)}(\vec{r})\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,E_{(n)}t/\hbar).

(1.16)

Each physical measurement only observes one single stationary state (wavefunction collapse)
.
Let
\{\psi_{(n)}\}_{n}
be orthonormalized, which is achievable.
Then the energy
\bar{E}=\sum_{n}\left\lvert c_{(n)}\right\rvert^{2}E_{(n)}
,
3
3

3

\bar{E}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\Psi^{*}(\vec{r},t)\hat{H}\Psi^{*}(\vec{r},t)=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\big(\sum_{n}c_{(n)}^{*}\psi_{(n)}^{*}(\vec{r})\exp(\mathrm{i}\,E_{(n)}t/\hbar)\big)\big(\sum_{n^{\prime}}E_{(n^{\prime})}c_{(n^{\prime})}^{*}\psi_{(n^{\prime})}(\vec{r})\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,E_{(n^{\prime})}t/\hbar)\big)=\sum_{n,n^{\prime}}c_{(n)}^{*}c_{(n^{\prime})}E_{(n^{\prime})}\exp(\mathrm{i}\,(E_{(n)}-E_{(n^{\prime})})t/\hbar)\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\psi_{(n)}^{*}(\vec{r})\psi_{(n^{\prime})}(\vec{r})=\sum_{n,n^{\prime}}c_{(n)}^{*}c_{(n^{\prime})}E_{(n^{\prime})}\exp(\mathrm{i}\,(E_{(n)}-E_{(n^{\prime})})t/\hbar)\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}
.

so
\left\lvert c_{(n)}\right\rvert^{2}
is the probability to be in state
n
.

1.1.4
Analytical mechanics origin: Schrödinger’s intuition

Lagrangian mechanics and the least action principle

For
N
particles with general coordinates
\vec{\bm{r}}:=(\vec{r}_{1},\cdots,\vec{r}_{N})
moving in potential
V(\vec{\bm{r}},t)
with kinetic energy
T(\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)
, define their
Lagrangian
, and the
action
along a curve
(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t})_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}
as:

\displaystyle L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t):=T(\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)-V(\vec{\bm{r}},t),\qquad\mathcal{S}[(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t})_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}]:=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\,L(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}_{t},t).

(1.17)

Fixing the ending points of all curves, the dynamics between the two points,
i.e.
, the curve
(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t})_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}
that the particles move along, is given by the
least action principle
as the
extremal curve
.
Explicitly, it is given by the
Euler-Lagrange equation (E-L equation)
(Euler, 1753; Lagrange, 1754; Euler, 1766):
4
4

4

For Eq. (
),
\updelta\mathcal{S}=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\,\left\lparen\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}+\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}\right\rparen=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\,\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\lparen\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}\right\rparen-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\,\left\lparen\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\right\rparen\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}=\left.\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}\right|_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\,\left\lparen\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\right\rparen\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}
.

\displaystyle\updelta\mathcal{S}=\left.\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}\right|_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t\,\left\lparen\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\right\rparen\cdot\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}=0,\quad\forall\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}\text{~s.t.~}\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t_{1}}=\updelta\vec{\bm{r}}_{t_{2}}=0,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L=0,\forall\text{ a.e. }t\in[t_{1},t_{2}].\ignorespaces\ignorespaces

For classical particles and Euclidean coordinates,
T(\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)=\sum_{i}\frac{1}{2}m_{i}\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}^{2}
, which recovers Newton’s law,
m_{i}\ddot{\vec{r}}_{i}=-\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}V
.

Noether’s theorem

Uniformities lead to conservatives (1918).

•

Uniformity in space: the replacement
\vec{\bm{r}}\mapsto\vec{\bm{r}}+\mathrm{\Delta}\vec{r},\forall\mathrm{\Delta}\vec{r}
gives the same Lagrangian. So
\mathrm{\Delta}L=\sum_{i}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}L\cdot\mathrm{\Delta}\vec{r}=0,\Longrightarrow\sum_{i}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}L=0
so
V
is uniform, and
\mathrel{{\mathop{\Longrightarrow}\limits}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sum_{i}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}}L=0
,
so the (total)
momentum

\vec{P}:=\sum_{i}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}}L
is a conservative.

•

Uniformity in time: the replacement
t\mapsto t+\mathrm{\Delta}t,\forall\mathrm{\Delta}t
gives the same Lagrangian. So
\mathrm{\Delta}L=\partial_{t}L\mathrm{\Delta}t=0,\Longrightarrow\partial_{t}L=0
so
L
thus
V
and
T
does not explicitly depend on time, so
\frac{\mathrm{d}L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}})}{\mathrm{d}t}=\sum_{i}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}L\cdot\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}+\sum_{i}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}}L\cdot\ddot{\vec{r}}_{i},\mathrel{{\mathop{\Longrightarrow}\limits}}\frac{\mathrm{d}L}{\mathrm{d}t}=\sum_{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\nabla_{\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}}L\cdot\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}\right),\Longrightarrow\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\sum_{i}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}}L\cdot\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}-L\right)=0
,
so the
energy

E:=\sum_{i}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}}L\cdot\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}-L
is a conservative.

Hamiltonian mechanics

Formulation based on energy (conservative) thus superior to Lagrangian, excels in applicability to mechanics problems.
Define the (general)
momentum

\vec{p}_{i}:=\nabla_{\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}}L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)
,
and the
Hamiltonian
as the Legendre transform of the Lagrangian
L
in argument
\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}
:

\displaystyle H(\vec{\bm{r}},\vec{\bm{p}},t):=\sup_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}\Big(\sum_{i}\vec{p}_{i}\cdot\dot{\vec{r}}_{i}-L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)\Big)=\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}-L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)\Big|_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}=\vec{\bm{v}}(\vec{\bm{r}},\vec{\bm{p}},t)\text{ solved from }\vec{\bm{p}}=\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)},

(1.18)

which takes
\vec{\bm{p}}
as the basic/free variable in place of
\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}
.
Then we have:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}H=\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\vec{\bm{v}}-\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\vec{\bm{v}}=-\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L,\\
\nabla_{\vec{\bm{p}}}H=\vec{\bm{v}}+\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\bm{p}}}\vec{\bm{v}}-\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\bm{p}}}\vec{\bm{v}}=\vec{\bm{v}},\\
\partial_{t}H=\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\partial_{t}\vec{\bm{v}}-\partial_{t}L-\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\partial_{t}\vec{\bm{v}}=-\partial_{t}L.\end{cases}

(1.19)

The E-L Eq. (
) becomes
\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\vec{\bm{p}}=0
,
i.e.
,
\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L=\dot{\vec{\bm{p}}}
.
This gives the
canonical equation
/
Hamilton equation
:

\displaystyle\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}=\nabla_{\vec{\bm{p}}}H,\quad\dot{\vec{\bm{p}}}=-\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}H.

(1.20)

Alternatively, when
L
does not explicitly depend on time, the total differential

\mathrm{d}H=\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}+\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\vec{\bm{p}}-\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L\cdot\mathrm{d}\vec{\bm{r}}-\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\mathrm{d}\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}=\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}+\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\vec{\bm{p}}-\dot{\vec{\bm{p}}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\vec{\bm{r}}-\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}=\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\vec{\bm{p}}-\dot{\vec{\bm{p}}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\vec{\bm{r}}

also gives Eq. (
1.20
).
In this case,
H(\vec{\bm{r}},\vec{\bm{p}})\equiv E
also does not explicitly depend on time and is conserved.

Hamilton-Jacobi equation

Consider the action in Hamilton’s formulation. Define
Hamilton’s principal function
:

\displaystyle S(\vec{\bm{r}},t;\vec{\bm{r}}_{0}):=\min_{(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}_{\tau})_{\tau\in[0,t]}:\vec{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}=\vec{\bm{r}}_{0},\vec{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}=\vec{\bm{r}}}\mathcal{S}[(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}_{\tau})_{\tau}]=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\,L(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{\tau},\dot{\vec{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\star}_{\tau},\tau),

(1.21)

i.e.
, the least action between
\vec{\bm{r}}
at time
t
and some given
\vec{\bm{r}}_{0}
at time
0
, where
(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{\tau})_{\tau}
is the extremal curve between the two sets of points.

(1)
From Eq. (
),
\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}S=\left.\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{\tau}\right|_{0}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\left\lparen\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\right\rparen\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{\tau}
, where the derivatives of
L
are evaluated at
(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{\tau},\dot{\vec{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\star}_{\tau},\tau)
at each
\tau
.
Since
(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{\tau})_{\tau}
is the extremal curve,
\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\equiv 0
,
and
\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{0}=0
,
so
\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}S=\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{t},\dot{\vec{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\star}_{t},t)\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\vec{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}^{\star}=\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{t},\dot{\vec{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\star}_{t},t)
,
which is
\vec{\bm{p}}
on the moving curve:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}S=\vec{\bm{p}}.

(1.22)

(2)
Consider a physical moving curve
\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}
.
Then we have
\frac{\mathrm{d}S(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t},t;\vec{\bm{r}}_{0})}{\mathrm{d}t}=L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)
.
5
5

5

The change of
\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}
by
t
yields the same extremal curve since
\vec{\bm{r}}_{t}
is a physical moving curve:

(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t+s},t+s)}_{\tau})_{\tau\in[0,t]}=(\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t},t)}_{\tau})_{\tau\in[0,t]}
,
so the integrand is a function only of
t
.
Alternatively,
\frac{\mathrm{d}S(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t},t;\vec{\bm{r}}_{0})}{\mathrm{d}t}=L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\left\lparen\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}L-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\nabla_{\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}}L\right\rparen\cdot\partial_{t}\vec{\bm{\gamma}}^{\star}_{\tau}=L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)
.

On the other hand, formally,
\frac{\mathrm{d}S(\vec{\bm{r}}_{t},t;\vec{\bm{r}}_{0})}{\mathrm{d}t}=\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}S\cdot\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}+\partial_{t}S=\vec{\bm{p}}\cdot\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}}+\partial_{t}S
,
which is
H(\vec{\bm{r}},\vec{\bm{p}},t)+L(\vec{\bm{r}},\dot{\vec{\bm{r}}},t)+\partial_{t}S
by definition Eq. (
1.18
).
Then we arrive at the (time-dependent)
Hamilton-Jacobi equation (HJE)
:

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}S=H\Big(\vec{\bm{r}},\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}S,t\Big).

(1.23)

When the Hamiltonian does not explicitly depend on time
t
, it is a constant
E
, which leads to
\partial_{t}S=-E
,
\Longrightarrow S(\vec{\bm{r}},t)=S_{0}(\vec{\bm{r}})-Et
, and the time-independent Hamilton-Jacobi equation:

\displaystyle H\Big(\vec{\bm{r}},\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}S_{0}\Big)=E.

(1.24)

•

HJE: a single first-order PDE for a function of
N
coordinates and time.

E-L Eq.: time evolution of
N
coordinates;
N
equations; second-order.

Hamilton Eq.: time evolution of
2N
coordinates;
2N
equations; first-order.

•

HJE “is particularly useful in identifying conserved quantities for mechanical systems”.

•

HJE “is also the only formulation of mechanics in which the motion of a particle can be represented as a wave”, “analogy between the propagation of light and the motion of a particle”.

Connection to Schrödinger equation

HJE generalizes the duality between trajectories and wave fronts by the variational principle in geometrical optics to mechanical systems:

\displaystyle\text{wave-front (isosurface of $S(\vec{\bm{r}},t)$) }\underset{\text{HJE}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightleftharpoons}\limits}}}\text{ trajectory}.

(1.25)

So we can treat
S
as the phase of a wave:
\Psi(\vec{\bm{r}},t)=A\exp(\mathrm{i}\,S(\vec{\bm{r}},t)/\hbar)
(let
S
be complex to allow change of amplitude),
which leads to:
S(\vec{\bm{r}},t)=-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\log\Psi(\vec{\bm{r}},t)+\mathrm{const}
. So:

\displaystyle\vec{\bm{p}}\Psi=\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}S\Psi=-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\Psi,

(1.26)

which explains the momentum operator
\hat{\vec{p}}=-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\nabla
.
HJE (
1.23
) then yields:

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\partial_{t}\Psi=H\Big(\vec{\bm{r}},-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\frac{1}{\Psi}\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\Psi,t\Big)\Psi,

(1.27)

which explains Schrödinger Eq. (
1.9
)
\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\partial_{t}\Psi=\hat{H}\Psi
.
For the time-independent case, we first have:

\Psi(\vec{\bm{r}},t)=A\exp(\mathrm{i}\,S_{0}(\vec{\bm{r}})/\hbar)\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,Et/\hbar)=:\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,Et/\hbar)
is separable.
By the time-independent HJE Eq. (
1.24
), we have:

\displaystyle H\Big(\vec{\bm{r}},-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\frac{1}{\psi}\nabla_{\vec{\bm{r}}}\psi\Big)\psi=E\psi,

(1.28)

which explains the stationary Schrödinger Eq. (
1.14
)
\hat{H}\psi=E\psi
.
For
H(\vec{\bm{r}},\vec{\bm{p}})=\sum_{i}\frac{\vec{p}_{i}^{2}}{2m_{i}}+V(\vec{\bm{r}})
, Eq. (
1.28
) becomes
0=\sum_{i}\frac{1}{2m_{i}}\left\lparen-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\frac{1}{\psi}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi\right\rparen^{*}\cdot\left\lparen-\mathrm{i}\,\hbar\frac{1}{\psi}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi\right\rparen\psi+(V-E)\psi=\sum_{i}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{i}}\frac{\psi}{\left\lvert\psi\right\rvert^{2}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi\right\rVert^{2}+(V-E)\psi
.
Multiplied by
\psi^{*}
, it becomes
\sum_{i}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{i}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi\right\rVert^{2}+(V-E)\left\lvert\psi\right\rvert^{2}=\sum_{i}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{i}}\left\lparen\left\lVert\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}u\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}v\right\rVert^{2}\right\rparen+(V-E)(u^{2}+v^{2})=0
, where
u
,
v
denote the real, imaginary parts of
\psi
, which are independent
\mathbb{R}^{3N}\to\mathbb{R}
functions.
So the variational principle of its integral is applied for
u
and
v
separately, yielding
2(V-E)u-\sum_{i}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\cdot\left\lparen 2\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}u\right\rparen=0
and similarly for
v
.
Written combined, the result is:

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{i}}\nabla^{2}_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi+V\psi=E\psi,

(1.29)

which explains the Hamilton operator
\hat{H}=-\sum_{i}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{i}}\nabla^{2}_{i}+V
.
6
6

6

Another intuition:
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{i}\,\big\lVert\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi\big\rVert^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{i}\,\nabla_{i}\psi^{*}\cdot\nabla_{i}\psi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{i}\,\nabla_{i}\cdot(\psi^{*}\nabla_{i}\psi)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{i}\,\psi^{*}\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{i}\,\psi^{*}\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\cdot\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi
,
if
\psi
diminishes sufficiently fast to make
\oint_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\vec{S}_{i}\cdot(\psi^{*}\nabla_{i}\psi)=0
.

Adopting the general complex derivative Eq. (
0.2
), Eq. (
1.29
) can also be seen as derived from:

\displaystyle\frac{\updelta}{\updelta\psi^{*}}\Big(\sum_{i}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{i}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\vec{r}_{i}}\psi\right\rVert^{2}+(V-E)\left\lvert\psi\right\rvert^{2}\big)=0.

(1.30)

Note that the eigen-energy as the solution to Eq. (
1.14
) must be real,
otherwise the wavefunction
\chi(t)=A\exp(-\mathrm{i}\,Et/\hbar)
would go to infinity for large
t
.
Other physical quantities also must be real, since physical measurement is real. So,

Any mechanical quantity must be real

(1.31)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

All eigenvalues of the corresponding operator is real

(1.32)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle\text{The operator is {Hermitian (self-adjoint)}: }\langle\phi|\hat{O}\psi\rangle=\langle\hat{O}\phi|\psi\rangle.

(1.33)

1.2
Basic Conclusions

Locally integrable functions have weak derivative.
Note the test functions in defining the weak derivative is compactly supported.

The
N
-particle wavefunction
\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
has zero boundary integral to make the kinetic energy operator
\hat{T}
Hermitian:

\langle\psi|\hat{T}\phi\rangle=\langle\psi|\hat{T}|\phi\rangle=-\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\psi^{*}\nabla_{i}\cdot(\nabla_{i}\phi)=-\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\nabla_{i}\cdot(\psi^{*}\nabla_{i}\phi)+\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\nabla_{i}\psi^{*}\cdot\nabla_{i}\phi
,
and symmetrically

\langle\hat{T}\psi|\phi\rangle=\langle\phi|\hat{T}|\psi\rangle^{*}=-\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\nabla_{i}\cdot(\phi\nabla_{i}\psi^{*})+\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\nabla_{i}\phi\cdot\nabla_{i}\psi^{*}
.
To make them equal,
0=\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\big(\nabla_{i}\cdot(\psi^{*}\nabla_{i}\phi)-\nabla_{i}\cdot(\phi\nabla_{i}\psi^{*})\big)=\sum_{i}\sum_{s_{i}}\lim_{r_{i}\to\infty}\oint_{\mathbb{S}(r_{i})}\mathrm{d}\vec{S}\cdot\big(\psi^{*}\nabla_{i}\phi-\phi\nabla_{i}\psi^{*}\big)
for any
\psi
and
\phi
, so each term has to be zero.
We then have
\langle\psi|\hat{T}|\psi\rangle=\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\left\lVert\nabla_{i}\psi\right\rVert^{2}
.

\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
is in the
(1,2)
-Sobolev space
\mathbb{H}^{1}
(
\psi
and all its 1st-order weak derivatives are in
\mathbb{L}^{2}
; it is a Hilbert space)
[
14
]
, in order to make kinetic energy finite.

The ground state is spacially non-degenerate. If the Hamiltonian contains spin, the ground state seems non-degenerate ([
ref. thesis
, Appendix A]).

DFT initialization methods:
[
ref. webpage
]

Natural orbitals: eigenvectors the 1-RDM of a N-wavefunction.
Natural atomic orbital: atomic orbitals of an atom in a molecular environment. Can be constructed from free-atom natural atomic orbitals.
[
ref. webpage
].

Hückel method: assume
\pi
-bond MOs are the linear combinations of (unhybridized) p-atomic-orbitals of involved atoms.
[
ref. webpage
].
Extended Hückel method: also consider
\sigma
-bonds [
Wikipedia
][Hoffmann 1963],
which leverages the Wolfsberg-Helmholz approximation [1952] for off-diagonal Hamiltonian elements, and uses approximate valence state ionization potentials (IPs) for diagonal elements.

Differential virial theorem and implication to
E_{\overline{\textnormal{XC}}}
:
[
9
]
.

Mixed state vs. superposition of eigenstates

“The density matrix was first introduced by von Neumann (von Neumann 1927) and Landau (Landau 1927) independently to describe the quantum mechanical natures of statistical systems.”
[
19
]
.

([
ref. post
],
[
ref. webpage
])

Let
\{\left\lvert\psi_{(1)}\right\rangle,\left\lvert\psi_{(2)}\right\rangle\}
be orthonormal eigenstates of observable
\hat{A}
.
Then the equally-weighted
superposition state
,
\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\left\lvert\psi_{(1)}\right\rangle+\left\lvert\psi_{(2)}\right\rangle)
, is a pure state, meaning that
“there is not a 50% chance the system is in state
\left\lvert\psi_{(1)}\right\rangle
and 50% in state
\left\lvert\psi_{(2)}\right\rangle
, but there is a 0% chance that the system is in either state, and a 100% chance the system is in state
\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle
;
the point is that these statements are all made before making any measurements.”

Although both the superposition state, whose density matrix is
\Gamma_{[\psi]}=\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle\left\langle\psi\right\rvert=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}
,
and the equally-weighted
mixed state
of the two pure states
\left\lvert\psi_{(1)}\right\rangle
and
\left\lvert\psi_{(2)}\right\rangle
, whose density matrix is
\Gamma_{\text{mix}}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
,
give the same
\hat{A}
measurement result of 50%
A_{(1)}
and 50%
A_{(2)}
,
they differ when measuring a second observable
\hat{B}
s.t.
[\hat{A},\hat{B}]\neq 0
.
Let
\{\left\lvert\phi_{(1)}\right\rangle,\left\lvert\phi_{(2)}\right\rangle\}
be orthonormal eigenstates of
\hat{B}
and suppose
\left\lvert\psi_{(1)}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\left\lvert\phi_{(1)}\right\rangle+\left\lvert\phi_{(2)}\right\rangle)
and
\left\lvert\psi_{(2)}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\left\lvert\phi_{(1)}\right\rangle-\left\lvert\phi_{(2)}\right\rangle)
(the relation must not be diagonal due to the non-commutability).
The superposition state
\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle=\left\lvert\phi_{(1)}\right\rangle
so it gives 100%
B_{(1)}
and 0%
B_{(2)}
measurement result,
while the mixed state
\Gamma_{\text{mix}}
takes the same matrix form under the
\{\phi_{(1)},\phi_{(2)}\}
basis so it still gives 50%
B_{(1)}
and 50%
B_{(2)}
measurement result.

A general
density operator
is a trace-one linear kernel (positive semi-definite Hermitian operator) on the Hilbert space of quantum states, and satisfies proper symmetry in many-body cases.
The spectral theorem of kernels gives it a universal expression:

\displaystyle\exists\text{ orthonormal }\{\left\lvert\kappa\right\rangle\}_{\kappa},\text{ real non-negative }\{\lambda_{\kappa}\}_{\kappa}\text{ with }\sum_{\kappa}\lambda_{\kappa}=1,\text{~s.t.~}\Gamma=\sum_{\kappa}\lambda_{\kappa}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rangle\left\langle\kappa\right\rvert.

(1.34)

For a pure state,
\bar{A}=\langle\psi|\hat{A}|\psi\rangle=\tr\big(\hat{A}\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle\left\langle\psi\right\rvert\big)=\tr(\hat{A}\Gamma_{[\psi]})
,
so we can extend the measurement to be taken under a general density matrix state:

\displaystyle\bar{A}=\tr(\hat{A}\Gamma).

(1.35)

For a complete basis
\{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\}_{\alpha}
, let
\mathbf{S}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\alpha\right\rangle
be the overlap matrix (or, Gram matrix).
Then for any vector
\left\lvert v\right\rangle
, its expansion is
\left\lvert v\right\rangle=\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{v}^{\alpha}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle
, where
\mathbf{v}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}(\mathbf{S}^{-1})^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\langle\beta\middle|v\right\rangle
,
i.e.
, the projection operator is
\sum_{\alpha\beta}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle(\mathbf{S}^{-1})^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\langle\beta\right\rvert
.
An observable operator has a common matrix expression:

\displaystyle\hat{A}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert,\quad\text{where }\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{S}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{S}^{-1},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}:=\langle\alpha|\hat{A}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle,

(1.36)

and so does the density matrix:

\displaystyle\Gamma=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\beta}_{\beta^{\prime}}\left\lvert\beta\right\rangle\left\langle\beta^{\prime}\right\rvert,\quad\text{where }\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{S}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\mathbf{S}^{-1},\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}:=\langle\alpha|\hat{\Gamma}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle.

(1.37)

This gives:

\displaystyle\bar{A}={}

\displaystyle\tr(\hat{A}\Gamma)=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{S}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta}\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\beta}_{\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{S}^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\alpha}=\tr(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{\Gamma}\mathbf{S})=\tr(\mathbf{S}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}})=\tr(\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}})=\tr(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{\Gamma}).

(1.38)

If the basis is orthonormal, then
\tr(\hat{A}\Gamma)=\tr(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\Gamma})=\tr(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}})
.
If using the spectral basis
\{\left\lvert\kappa\right\rangle\}_{\kappa}
of
\Gamma
in which case
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\Diag(\bm{\lambda})
, then
\tr(\hat{A}\Gamma)=\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{\kappa}\lambda_{\kappa}\langle\kappa|\hat{A}|\kappa\rangle
.

That a matrix cannot be written as a vector outer-product means (1) it is an entangled state if the matrix represents the state of multiple particles/qubits under the product basis, or (2) it is a mixed state if the matrix is a density matrix.

Misc

If an operator
\hat{A}
does not contain spins (or does not operates on spins), then its spin-basis density matrix is a scalar matrix:
\mathbf{A}^{s,\alpha}_{s^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}=\delta^{s}_{s^{\prime}}\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.

Any unitary operator
\hat{U}
can be expressed by an Hermitian operator
\hat{H}
in the way
\hat{U}=e^{\mathrm{i}\,\hat{H}}
. Alternatively,
\{\mathrm{i}\,\hat{H}\}
is the Lie algebra of the Lie group
\{\hat{U}\}
.

Tiling theorem
[
1
,
4
]
: For
N
fermions, all the ground-state nodal pockets belong to the same class (can be produced by permuting one nodal pocket).
Roughly means besides the boundary and the case
\mathrm{x}_{i}=\mathrm{x}_{j}
,
\psi_{(0)}\neq 0
a.s.

2
Quantum Many-Body Systems

2.1
General Setup and Conclusions

2.1.1
Wavefunction, density and density matrix

For
N
electrons each with coordinate
\mathrm{x}_{i}:=(\vec{r}_{i},s_{i})
where
s_{i}\in\left\{-\frac{1}{2},+\frac{1}{2}\right\}
or
s_{i}\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}
is its spin,
denote their wavefunction as
\psi(\mathbf{x})=\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
(may also denoted as
\langle\mathbf{x}|\psi\rangle
).

•

Antisymmetry:
\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{i},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{j},\cdots)=-\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{j},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{i},\cdots)
.

This indicates
\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x},\cdots,\mathrm{x},\cdots)=-\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x},\cdots,\mathrm{x},\cdots)=0
.

•

Inner product:
\left\langle\phi\middle|\psi\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\phi^{*}(\mathbf{x})\psi(\mathbf{x})=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\phi^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
.

•

Normalization condition:
\left\langle\psi\middle|\psi\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}=1
.

•

Joint density
:
\left\lvert\psi(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}
.

(n)
The joint density normalizes to
1
, according to the normalization condition of the wavefunction.

Due to the wavefunction antisymmetry,

(s)

\left\lvert\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{i},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{j},\cdots)\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{j},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{i},\cdots)\right\rvert^{2}
:
the joint density is
symmetric
(the electrons are indistinguishable);

(b)

\left\lvert\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x},\cdots,\mathrm{x},\cdots)\right\rvert^{2}=0
:
events that “electron
i
is at
\mathrm{x}
” and that “electron
j
is at
\mathrm{x}
” for
i\neq j
are
mutually exclusive
, meaning the
Pauli exclusion principle
.

•

Density
:

\rho(\mathrm{x}):=N\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{2:N}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rvert^{2}
.
May also denoted as
\rho_{[\psi]}(\mathrm{x})
,
\rho^{(1)}_{[\psi]}(\mathrm{x})
, or
\rho_{1}
.

It is not the usual probability density function, but the
electron number density
that takes all the
N
electrons into account.
Due to Pauli exclusion principle and the symmetry of
\left\lvert\psi(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}
, we have:

\rho(\mathrm{x})=\sum_{i\in[N]}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{\neg i}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{i}=\mathrm{x},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rvert^{2}=\sum_{i\in[N]}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{\neg 1}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1}=\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rvert^{2}=N\int\mathbf{x}_{\neg 1}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rvert^{2}
, as is defined here.

(n)
It normalizes to
\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\rho(\mathrm{x})=N
, the total number of electrons (can also be seen from the definition).

Define
N^{s}:=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\rho(\vec{r},s)=Np^{s}
, where
p^{s}:=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{2:N}\,\left\lvert\psi((\vec{r},s),\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rvert^{2}
.
Due to joint density symmetry, this
p^{s}
is the
same for all electrons
(independent of
i
).
Note
\sum_{s}p^{s}=1
, and
\sum_{s}N^{s}=N
.

•

Pair density
:

\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}):=N(N-1)\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{3:N}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rvert^{2}
.
May also denoted as
\rho^{(2)}_{[\psi]}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})
or
\rho_{12}
.

Similar to the density, this pair density is the
electron-pair number density
of firstly finding an electron at
\mathrm{x}_{1}
and then finding another electron at
\mathrm{x}_{2}
.
Again due to Pauli exclusion principle and joint-density symmetry,

\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1}^{\star},\mathrm{x}_{2}^{\star})=\sum_{i\neq j}\int\mathbf{x}_{\neg i,\neg j}\,\left\lvert\psi(\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{i}=\mathrm{x}_{1}^{\star},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{j}=\mathrm{x}_{2}^{\star},\cdots)\right\rvert^{2}=\sum_{i\neq j}\int\mathbf{x}_{\neg 1,\neg 2}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1}=\mathrm{x}_{1}^{\star},\mathrm{x}_{2}=\mathrm{x}_{2}^{\star},\cdots)\right\rvert^{2}=N(N-1)\int\mathbf{x}_{\neg 1,\neg 2}\,\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1}=\mathrm{x}_{1}^{\star},\mathrm{x}_{2}=\mathrm{x}_{2}^{\star},\cdots)\right\rvert^{2}
, as is defined here.

(n)
It normalizes to
N(N-1)
, the total number of ordered electron pairs (can also be seen from the definition).

(m)
Marginalization yields
\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=(N-1)\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})
from the definitions.
This is to marginalize over other electrons, so the number of other electrons
N-1
is derived.

(i)
If two electrons are statistically independent, then
\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\frac{N-1}{N}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})
,
where the factor enters since given that one electron is found at
\mathrm{x}_{1}
, the conditional electron density of another electron normalizes to
N-1
.

(s)
Pair density is symmetric:
\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{1})
, due to the wavefunction antisymmetry,

(b)

Fermi hole
or
exchange hole
(or
Fermi/exchange correlation
):
\rho(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x})=0
.

This indicates a correlation between two
parallel-spin
electrons,
i.e.
any two
parallel-spin
electrons are
not independent
.
This correlation/hole comes from the antisymmetry of
\psi
, and only applies to parallel-spin electrons.
It does not rely on any interaction (
e.g.
, Coulomb potential), and is a global/distant correlation.
It is the “force” that keeps a neutron star from collapse under gravity, where there is no Coulomb repulsion. Like the electron, a neutron has spin
{}^{1}\!/_{\!2}
.

Spacial versions
:
\rho(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2}):=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s_{1}),(\vec{r}_{2},s_{2}))
,
\rho^{s}(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2}):=\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))
.
Then
\rho^{s}(\vec{r},\vec{r})=0
, but
\rho(\vec{r},\vec{r})
is unnecessarily zero.

•

One-particle reduced density matrix (1-RDM)
:

P(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}):=N\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{2:N}\,\psi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\psi^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
.
May also denoted as
P^{(1)}_{[\psi]}(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime})
,
\left\langle\mathrm{x}\middle|P^{(1)}\middle|\mathrm{x}^{\prime}\right\rangle
, or
P^{1}_{1^{\prime}}
.

Note that
P(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x})=\rho(\mathrm{x})
, and
P(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime})=P^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime};\mathrm{x})
.

•

Two-particle reduced density matrix (2-RDM)

[
19
,
11
]
:

P(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2}):=N(N-1)\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{3:N}\,\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\psi^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
.
May also denoted as
P^{(2)}_{[\psi]}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})
,
\left\langle\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}\middle|P^{(2)}\middle|\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2}\right\rangle
, or
P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}
.

Note that
P(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})
,

\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2}\,P(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})=(N-1)P(\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})
, and

P(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})=P^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2};\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})
,

P(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})=-P(\mathrm{x}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})=-P(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})=P(\mathrm{x}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})
.
Due to the Fermi/exchange hole, we have
P(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x})=\rho(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x})=0
.

2.1.2
Correlations and Hole Functions

Define the
conditional probability

\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1}):=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})/\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})
.

Again, it is actually the
conditional electron number density
of other electrons given that there is already one electron found at
\mathrm{x}_{1}
out of
N
electrons in total.

(n)
It normalizes to
\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1})=N-1
, the number of other electrons (see also Property (m) of pair density).

(i)
For independent electrons,
\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1})=\frac{N-1}{N}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})
.

Define the
correlation factor

f(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}):=\frac{\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})}{\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})}-1
.

(s)
It is symmetric.

(i)
For independent electrons,
f(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=-\frac{1}{N}
.

(1)

\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})(1+f(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}))
.

Define the
exchange-correlation hole function

h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1}):=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1})-\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})=\frac{\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})}{\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})}-\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})=\frac{\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})-\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})}{\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})}=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})f(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})
.

This definition is for an
N
-free description of two-electron correlation.

(n)
By definition, it normalizes to
\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1})=-1
, representing the removal of the given electron.

(b)
Since
\rho(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x})=0
, we have
h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\mathrm{x}|\mathrm{x})=-\rho(\mathrm{x})
.

(i)
For independent electrons,
h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1})=-\frac{1}{N}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})
.

(1)
By definition,
\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})+\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1})
.

Define the
spin-independent exchange-correlation hole function
(or the
total hole function
)

h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1}):=\frac{\rho(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2})}{\rho(\vec{r}_{1})}-\rho(\vec{r}_{2})=\frac{\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s_{1}),(\vec{r}_{2},s_{2}))}{\sum_{s^{\prime}_{1}}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})}-\sum_{s^{\prime}_{2}}\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s^{\prime}_{2})=\frac{\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s_{1}),(\vec{r}_{2},s_{2}))-\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s_{1})\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s_{2})}{\sum_{s^{\prime}_{1}}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})}
.

Note that
h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})\neq\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2},s_{2}|\vec{r}_{1},s_{1})=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\frac{\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s_{1}),(\vec{r}_{2},s_{2}))-\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s_{1})\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s_{2})}{\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s_{1})}
in general.

(n)
It normalizes to
\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\frac{(N-1)\rho(\vec{r}_{1})}{\rho(\vec{r}_{1})}-N=-1
.

(i)
For independent electrons,
h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=-\frac{1}{N}\rho(\vec{r}_{2})
(since
\rho(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2})=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\frac{N-1}{N}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})=\rho(\vec{r}_{1})\frac{N-1}{N}\rho(\vec{r}_{2})
).

(1)
By definition,
\rho(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2})=\rho(\vec{r}_{1})\rho(\vec{r}_{2})+\rho(\vec{r}_{1})h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
.

The Fermi hole and the Coulomb hole

The total hole
h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
can be decomposed of two components:

\displaystyle h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})+h_{\textnormal{C}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1}).

(2.1)

The
Fermi hole

h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
is “due to the Pauli exclusion principle,
i.e.
, the antisymmetry of the wavefunction, and applies only to electrons with the same spin”
[
11
]
.
It describes the correlation between the given electron at
\vec{r}_{1}
and other electrons with the same spin as the given electron.

(n)
It normalizes to
\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=-1
, since excluding the given electron reduces the number of electrons with the same spin by 1.
“By this removal of one charge, the Fermi hole also takes care of the self-interaction problem”
[
11
]
.

(b)
Since the two electrons have the same spin, when they have the same spacial coordinate, the hole should reduce the density to zero. So
h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{1}|\vec{r}_{1})=-\rho(\vec{r}_{1})
.

(1)
“
h_{\textnormal{X}}
is negative everywhere,
h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})<0
”
[
11
]
.

(2)

h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
for the
\mathrm{H}_{2}
molecule is independent of
\vec{r}_{1}
. Also, it is then delocalized.

(3)

h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
is not spherically symmetric in
\vec{r}_{2}
.
It “stays behind” in the normal high electron density regions when
\vec{r}_{1}
goes outside the regions
[
11
]
.

The
Coulomb hole

h_{\textnormal{C}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
is due to dynamical interaction between electrons.

(n)
Due to the normalization of
h_{\textnormal{XC}}
and
h_{\textnormal{X}}
, it normalizes to
\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,h_{\textnormal{C}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=0
.
It is natural since excluding the given electron does not change the number of electrons with a different spin.

(1)
It is thus positive in some regions and negative in others, and may also be delocalized.

(2)
Since this hole is due to physical interaction,
h_{\textnormal{C}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
must change with
\vec{r}_{1}
.

However, the exact definition of the two holes at this generality that satisfies the mentioned properties is not found.

(I)
The first possibility is
h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\big(\sum_{s}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))-\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s)\big)/\rho(\vec{r}_{1})
,
and
h_{\textnormal{C}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\big(\sum_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s^{\prime}))-\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s^{\prime})\big)/\rho(\vec{r}_{1})
.
This may best fit the conceptual definition, and complies with the HF case
h_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=h_{\textnormal{X}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
(with A2.2; see Eq. (
3.15
)).
But it does not naturally satisfy Property (n)
\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\big(\sum_{s}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))-N^{s}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\big)/\big(\sum_{s}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\big)\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}-1

nor Property (b)
h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{1}|\vec{r}_{1})=-\big(\sum_{s}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)^{2}\big)/\big(\sum_{s}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\big)\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}-\sum_{s}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)
.

(II)
Another guess is
h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\big(\sum_{s}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))\big)/\rho(\vec{r}_{1})-\rho(\vec{r}_{2})
,
and
h_{\textnormal{C}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\big(\sum_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s^{\prime}))\big)/\rho(\vec{r}_{1})
.
This satisfies Property (b)
h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{1}|\vec{r}_{1})=-\rho(\vec{r}_{1})
, but does not comply with the HF case
h_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})\neq h_{\textnormal{X}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
(even with A2.2)
nor Property (n)
\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\big(\sum_{s}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))\big)/\rho(\vec{r}_{1})-N\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}-1
.

(III)
Perhaps
h_{\textnormal{X}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
can be defined as
h_{\textnormal{X}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
(see Eq. (
3.15
)), the total hole in the HF case under A2.2 (
i.e.
, sum over parallel spins only), which only takes the exchange correlation into consideration.
This seems adopted by [ref.
webpage1
,
webpage2
] and may also be the way to define
h_{\textnormal{X}}
as
h_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\lambda=0}
in the adiabatic connection.
But the correspondence between the orbitals
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
and a general
N
-electron wavefunction is undetermined (effective potential is unknown), at least not explicit.
Even in an HF system where the Slater determinant is exact, Properties (n) and (b) do not seem to hold (see Eq. (
3.11
)).

(IV)
Maybe the definition of the Fermi and Coulomb holes should not follow the spin-independent pair density.
The usage of the holes comes from the exchange-correlation energy
E_{\textnormal{XC}}:=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\frac{\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})-\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})}{r_{12}}
(Eq. (
2.94
)), where

\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})-\rho(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho(\mathrm{x}_{2})=\big(\sum_{s}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))-\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s)\big)+\big(\sum_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s^{\prime}))-\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s^{\prime})\big)=:\sum_{s}\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)\big(h_{\textnormal{X}}^{s}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})+h_{\textnormal{C}}^{s}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})\big)
, where we have defined

h_{\textnormal{X}}^{s}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1}):=h_{\textnormal{XC}}((\vec{r}_{2},s)|(\vec{r}_{1},s))=\frac{\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))}{\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)}-\rho(\vec{r}_{2},s)
, and

h_{\textnormal{C}}^{s}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1}):=h_{\textnormal{XC}}((\vec{r}_{2},\neg s)|(\vec{r}_{1},s))=\frac{\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},\neg s))}{\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)}-\rho(\vec{r}_{2},\neg s)
.
This
h_{\textnormal{X}}^{s}
satisfies the counterpart of Property (b)
h_{\textnormal{X}}^{s}(\vec{r}_{1}|\vec{r}_{1})=-\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)
, and complies with the HF case
h_{\textnormal{C}}^{s}\equiv 0
for either
s
(with A2.2),
But it is unknown for Property (n)
\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,h_{\textnormal{X}}^{s}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})=\big(\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\rho((\vec{r}_{1},s),(\vec{r}_{2},s))\big)/\rho(\vec{r}_{1},s)-N^{s}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}-1
.
It also does not satisfy Eq. (
2.1
), though the spin-dependent counterpart holds:
h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1})=\mathbbl{1}_{s_{2}=s_{1}}h_{\textnormal{X}}^{s_{1}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})+\mathbbl{1}_{s_{2}\neq s_{1}}h_{\textnormal{C}}^{s_{1}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
.
It seems to match
Koch and Holthausen [11, Eq. (2-20)]
, but cannot make the form
E_{\textnormal{XC}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\frac{\rho(\vec{r}_{1})h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})}{r_{12}}
.

Note that the above concepts and conclusions are general.
The Fermi (/Coulomb) hole describes any correlation between any two fermions of the same kind with the same spin (/with different spins) and the conclusions hold in general,
whatever the interaction is between the two fermions.
The Coulomb hole gets its name from the common case where the correlation between antiparallel-spin electrons mainly comes from their Coulomb (electrostatic) interaction.
The
total hole
is named exchange-correlation hole, since for interacting electrons, it “describes the change in conditional probability caused by the correlation for self-interaction, exchange and Coulomb correlation, compared to the completely uncorrelated situation”
[
11
]
.
To determine the holes, inter-fermion interaction and global potential (
i.e.
, the Hamiltonian) need to be specified, but in any setup, the conclusions here hold.

2.1.3
Operators

For a general one-particle operator
\hat{O}_{\cdot}
and a general two-particle operator
\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}
, define their extensions
\hat{O}
,
\hat{W}
to an
N
-particle wavefunction as:

\displaystyle\hat{O}\psi(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{k\in[N]}\hat{O}_{k}\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N}),\qquad\hat{W}\psi(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\neq k}\hat{W}_{jk}\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N}).

(2.2)

If
\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})
is (anti)symmetric, then:

\displaystyle\langle\psi|\hat{O}|\psi\rangle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N]}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\psi^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\hat{O}_{k}\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N]}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{k}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{\neg k}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\psi^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{k},\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{\neg k},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\hat{O}_{k}\psi(\mathrm{x}_{k},\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{\neg k},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N]}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\psi^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\hat{O}_{1}\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.5)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle N\langle\psi|\hat{O}_{1}|\psi\rangle,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\langle\psi|\hat{O}|\psi\rangle={}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\,\hat{O}_{1}P^{(1)}_{[\psi]}(\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathrm{x}_{1})=\tr\big(\hat{O}_{\cdot}P^{(1)}_{[\psi]}\big),

(2.7)

where (*) is due to that
\hat{O}
is linear thus commutes with the sign, and (#) is just a rename of dummy variables. Similarly,

\displaystyle\langle\psi|\hat{W}|\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}N(N-1)\langle\psi|\hat{W}_{12}|\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\tr\big(\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}P^{(2)}_{[\psi]}\big).

(2.8)

2.2
Second Quantization

[
ref. course note
].

2.2.1
Ladder operators: Algebraic description

Figure 1:
The ladder operators make things easier. [
source
]

Let
\hat{a}
be an operator on an arbitrary Hilbert space of states, and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}
be its conjugate.
Then
\hat{a}
is called a
lowering/annihilation operator
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}
a
raising/creation/destruction operator
),
if
[\hat{a},\hat{a}^{\dagger}]=1
(identity operator).
Jointly,
\hat{a}
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}
are called
ladder operators
.
The eigenstates of
\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}
satisfy:

\displaystyle[\hat{a},\hat{a}^{\dagger}]=1~~\Longrightarrow~~\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{}l}\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\,\left\lvert n\right\rangle&{}=n\,\left\lvert n\right\rangle,\\
\hat{a}^{\dagger}\,\left\lvert n\right\rangle&{}=\sqrt{n+1}\,\left\lvert n+1\right\rangle,\\
\hat{a}\,\left\lvert n\right\rangle&{}=\sqrt{n}\,\left\lvert n-1\right\rangle,\end{array}\right.n\in\mathbb{N}.

For quantum harmonic oscillator, the Hamiltonian is
\hat{H}=\frac{\hat{p}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}x^{2}
.
We can choose
\hat{a}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{m\omega}{\hbar}}x+\mathrm{i}\,\frac{\hat{p}}{\sqrt{m\omega\hbar}}\right)
, then
[\hat{a},\hat{a}^{\dagger}]=1
, and
\hat{H}=\hbar\omega\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\frac{1}{2}\right)
(Fig.
1
).
The eigenstates are then given by those of the ladder operator.

2.2.2
The Fock space

Useful conclusions.

Let
\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}
be the set of permutations / symmetric group on
[N]:=\{1,\dots,N\}
, and
\mathfrak{p}\in\{-1,1\}
be the particle parity.
If
\mathfrak{p}=-1
(fermion),
\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}
is the sign of permutation
\bm{\pi}
.
Let
\bigcirc
,
\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}
be two reducing operators (
e.g.
,
\sum
,
\prod
).

For a function
f(\cdot,\cdot)
that adopts two indices each within
[N]
, we have:

\displaystyle\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi},\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\bigcirc_{i\in[N]}f(\bm{\pi}_{i},\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{i})=N!\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\bigcirc_{i\in[N]}f(i,\bm{\pi}_{i}).

(2.12)

To see this, l.h.s is
\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi},\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\bigcirc_{j\in[N]}f(j,\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{\bm{\pi}^{-1}_{j}})=\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\circ\bm{\pi}^{-1}}\bigcirc_{j\in[N]}f(j,(\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\circ\bm{\pi}^{-1})_{j})
.
Since for a fixed
\bm{\pi}
,
\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\circ\bm{\pi}^{-1}
traverses
\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}
when
\bm{\pi}^{\prime}
does, so this is

\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}}\bigcirc_{j\in[N]}f(j,\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}_{j})=N!\,\framebox{$\phantom{o}$}_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}}\bigcirc_{j\in[N]}f(j,\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}_{j})
.

For a function
f(\cdot,\cdots,\cdot)
that adopts
N
(usually distinct) indices each within
[N]
, and a fixed position
k\in[N]
, we have:

\displaystyle\bigcirc_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}f(\bm{\pi}_{1},\cdots,\bm{\pi}_{N})=\bigcirc_{j\in[N]}\bigcirc_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}f(\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\underbrace{j}_{k\text{-th}},\cdots,\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N-1}).

(2.13)

To see this, for
j>k
, note that permuting
\bm{\pi}=(\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{k-1},j,\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{k},\cdots,\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N})
to
(1,\cdots,N)
whose sign is
\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}
, can be done by
first permuting
\bm{\pi}^{\prime}
to
(1,\cdots,k-1,k,\cdots,j-1,j+1,\cdots,N)
whose sign is
\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}
(kind of by definition),
and then permuting
(1,\cdots,k-1,j,k,\cdots,j-1,j+1,\cdots,N)
to
(1,\cdots,k-1,k,\cdots,j-1,j,j+1,\cdots,N)
whose sign is
\mathfrak{p}^{k-j}=\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}
.
Things go similarly for
j\leqslant k
.
So for such a
(\bm{\pi},\bm{\pi}^{\prime})
pair, we have
\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}=\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}
.
This leads to the determinant expansion along row/column
k
when
\mathfrak{p}=-1
.

Wavefunction/State description.

Given a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
of one-particle wavefunctions/states, the Fock space is the state space of multiple non-interacting identical/indistinguishable
\mathfrak{p}
-symmetric particles:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathscr{H}):=\overline{\bigoplus_{N=0}^{\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}(\mathscr{H})},\quad\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}(\mathscr{H}):=\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathscr{H}^{N}),

(2.14)

and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},0}:=\mathbb{C}
.
Here
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the
\mathfrak{p}
-symmetrizer, which constructs a Fock state from
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathscr{H}
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\left\lvert\phi_{1}\right\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\left\lvert\phi_{N}\right\rangle):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\,\left\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}\right\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\left\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}\right\rangle\quad\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}(\mathscr{H}),

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})=\big\langle\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N}\big|\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\big\rangle

(2.16)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{1}(\mathrm{x}_{\bm{\pi}_{1}})\cdots\phi_{N}(\mathrm{x}_{\bm{\pi}_{N}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\det\nolimits_{\mathfrak{p}}\left[\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{j})\right]_{ij}.

(2.17)

For bosons,
\mathfrak{p}=1
and this is a
permanent
.
For fermions,
\mathfrak{p}=-1
and this gives the
Slater determinant
Eq. (
3.2
).
By definition, we also have:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{k}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathfrak{p}^{k-1}\left\lvert\phi_{k}\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.18)

The inner product in
\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}
is:

\displaystyle\left\langle\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{N}\middle|\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\bm{\pi},\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\prod_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\chi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{i}}\middle|\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}\right\rangle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\prod_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\chi_{i}\middle|\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}\right\rangle=\det\nolimits_{\mathfrak{p}}\left[\left\langle\chi_{i}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\right]_{ij}.

(2.19)

For the second-quantization representation of a Fock state, let
\{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\}_{\alpha}
be a complete basis of
\mathscr{H}
.
Then a state
\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}
where
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq\{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\}_{\alpha}
can also be expressed by counting the number of
\phi_{i}
that occupies
\left\lvert 1\right\rangle,\left\lvert 2\right\rangle,\cdots
in turn:
\left\lvert n_{1},n_{2},\cdots\right\rangle
.
Due to the Pauli exclusion principle, for fermions, each
n_{i}
can only be either
0
or
1
.
So this expression is particularly interested for bosons, in which case each
\phi_{i}
occupying
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle
(
i.e.
,
\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle=\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle
) is called a
phonon
of type
\alpha
,
and
\left\lvert n_{1},n_{2},\cdots\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}!n_{2}!\cdots}}\lvert\underbrace{1\cdots 1}_{n_{1}}\underbrace{2\cdots 2}_{n_{2}}\cdots\rangle_{+1}
.

Reduced densities and RDMs of a Fock state.

For the reduced density matrices (RDMs) of a Fock state
\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}=\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}
, first note that:

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots\mathrm{x}_{N})\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{3}}(\mathrm{x}_{3})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{3})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N-1}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.21)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\left\lvert\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\sum_{k\in[N]\setminus\{j\}}\mathfrak{p}^{k-\mathbbl{1}_{k>j}-1}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j,k\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{3})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N-2}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.23)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(N-1)}}\sum_{j\neq k}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k+\mathbbl{1}_{k>j}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\left\lvert\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.24)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(N-1)}}\sum_{j<k}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k+1}\big(\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big)\left\lvert\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N}),

(2.25)

where
\mathbbl{1}_{k>j}
arises in (*) since if
k>j
, then
k-1
(instead of
k
) is the position (counted from 1) of the value
k
in the ordered sequence
[1,\cdots,j-1,j+1,\cdots,N]
of
\bm{\pi}^{\prime}
.
Since cavities are interchangeable, the symbol
\left\lvert\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}
means
\left\lvert\cdots\phi_{\neg\min\{j,k\}}\cdots\phi_{\neg\max\{j,k\}}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}
and is the same as
\left\lvert\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
So the 1-RDM and 2-RDM are:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}):={}&N\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots\mathrm{x}_{N})\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{x}_{2},\cdots\mathrm{x}_{N})\\
\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}&\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-j^{\prime}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x})\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime})\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}},\end{aligned}

(2.26)

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2}):=N(N-1)\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{3}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots\mathrm{x}_{N})\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2},\mathrm{x}_{3},\cdots\mathrm{x}_{N})

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\sum_{j^{\prime}\neq k^{\prime}}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k+\mathbbl{1}_{k>j}}\mathfrak{p}^{j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}+\mathbbl{1}_{k^{\prime}>j^{\prime}}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\phi_{\neg k^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\qquad

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j<k}\sum_{j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\mathfrak{p}^{j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}}\big(\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big)\big(\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)\cdots

(2.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\phi_{\neg k^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.30)

From Eq. (
2.26
), since
\mathfrak{p}^{j-j^{\prime}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x})\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x})\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}^{j^{\prime}-j}\phi_{j^{\prime}}(\mathrm{x})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x})\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}})^{*}
,
we have
\rho_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x})=\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x})\right\rvert^{2}\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}+2\sum_{j<j^{\prime}}\mathfrak{p}^{j-j^{\prime}}\Re\left\lparen\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x})\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x})\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\right\rparen
.

For
orthonormal

\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, from Eq. (
2.19
), we have
\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\delta_{j^{\prime}j}
,
and
\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\phi_{\neg k^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\delta_{j^{\prime}j}\delta_{k^{\prime}k}
for
j<k
and
j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}
. So:

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[N]}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}),

(2.31)

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j<k}\big(\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big)\big(\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)

(2.32)

\displaystyle={}
