\displaystyle\sum_{j<k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big(\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)+\sum_{j<k}\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big(\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)

(2.33)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j<k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big(\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)+\sum_{k<j}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big(\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)

(2.34)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big(\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)

(2.35)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big(\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)-\mathbbl{1}_{\mathfrak{p}=1}2\sum_{j\in[N]}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2}),

(2.36)

where (*) just renames
(k,j)\leftarrow(j,k)
for the second term.
Note that for fermions
\mathfrak{p}=-1
, the 2-RDM can be determined from the 1-RDM:

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{p}=-1}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})-\sum_{j,k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})

(2.37)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{p}=-1}(\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})P_{\mathfrak{p}=-1}(\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})-P_{\mathfrak{p}=-1}(\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})P_{\mathfrak{p}=-1}(\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1}).

(2.38)

Expressing 2-RDM using 1-RDM seems impossible for non-orthonormal
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
Note
\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\sum_{k\in[N]\setminus\{j\}}\mathfrak{p}^{(k-\mathbbl{1}_{k>j})-(k^{\prime}-\mathbbl{1}_{k^{\prime}>j^{\prime}})}\left\langle\phi_{k^{\prime}}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\phi_{\neg k^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

The density and pair-density functions are:

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x})\right\rvert^{2},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}+\mathfrak{p}\sum_{j,k}\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})-\mathbbl{1}_{\mathfrak{p}=1}2\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}

(2.40)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})+\mathfrak{p}\Big\lvert\sum_{j\in[N]}\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\Big\rvert^{2}-\mathbbl{1}_{\mathfrak{p}=1}2\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}.

(2.41)

2.2.3
Ladder operators in Fock space

Define a handy notation,
[\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}_{1}\ldots\bm{\pi}_{N}}
, as the tensor product where
\phi_{i}
appears in the
\bm{\pi}_{i}
-th position,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle[\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}_{1}\ldots\bm{\pi}_{N}}:={}

\displaystyle\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{-1}_{1}}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{-1}_{N}}\rangle,

(2.42)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N}\middle|[\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}_{1}\ldots\bm{\pi}_{N}}\right\rangle={}

\displaystyle[\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}_{1}\ldots\bm{\pi}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})=\phi_{1}(\mathrm{x}_{\bm{\pi}_{1}})\cdots\phi_{N}(\mathrm{x}_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}).

(2.43)

Since
\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}=\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{-1}}
and
\bm{\pi}^{-1}
traverses
\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}
when
\bm{\pi}
does, we have:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\,[\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}_{1}\ldots\bm{\pi}_{N}},

(2.44)

\displaystyle\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{k}\cdots\phi_{N}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\,[\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{k},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}\ldots j\ldots\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N-1}}

(2.45)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\,[\phi_{k},\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{\neg k},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{j,\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}\ldots\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N-1}}

(2.46)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\,[\phi_{k},\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}]_{j,(1,\ldots,\neg j,\ldots,N)}.

(2.47)

Given
\eta\in\mathscr{H}
, define the
creation operator

\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta):\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N-1}\to\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}
as:

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta)\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\left\lvert\eta\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\big[\eta,\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\big]_{j,(1,\ldots,\neg j,\ldots,N)}.

(2.48)

Its adjoint in
\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, the
annihilation operator

\hat{a}(\eta):\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N-1}\to\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}
, is characterized by

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\middle|\hat{a}(\eta)\middle|\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}={}

\displaystyle\left\langle\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{N}\middle|\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta)\middle|\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}

(2.49)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\left\langle\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{N}\middle|\big[\eta,\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\big]_{j,(1,\ldots,\neg j,\ldots,N)}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}

(2.50)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\sum_{k\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{k-j}\left\langle\chi_{k}\middle|\eta\right\rangle^{*}\left\langle\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{\neg k}\cdots\chi_{N}\middle|\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}

(2.51)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{k-1}\left\langle\eta\middle|\chi_{k}\right\rangle\left\langle\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N-1}\middle|\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{\neg k}\cdots\chi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}},

(2.52)

which indicates:

\displaystyle\hat{a}(\eta)\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\left\langle\eta\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.53)

Alternatively, the two operators can also be defined recursively, in the form presented in
Wikipedia
.
Define the
insertion and deletion operators
recursively:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\left\lvert 1\right\rangle:=\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle,\hskip 8.0pt

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}(\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rangle\otimes\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle):=\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle\otimes\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rangle\otimes\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle+\mathfrak{p}\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rangle\otimes(\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle);

(2.54)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle\oslash_{\mathfrak{p}}\left\lvert 1\right\rangle:=0,\hskip 8.0pt

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle\oslash_{\mathfrak{p}}(\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rangle\otimes\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle):=\delta_{ij}\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle+\mathfrak{p}\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rangle\otimes(\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rangle\oslash_{\mathfrak{p}}\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle).

(2.55)

Note
\otimes_{-1}
is just the wedge product
\wedge
.
Then the two operators can be defined as:

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta)\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N+1}}\left\lvert\eta\right\rangle\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle,\hskip 8.0pt\hat{a}(\eta)\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\lvert\eta\right\rangle\oslash_{\mathfrak{p}}\left\lvert\psi\right\rangle,\quad\forall\eta\in\mathscr{H},\psi\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}.

(2.56)

We can then verify that for any
\left\lvert\eta_{1}\right\rangle
and
\left\lvert\eta_{2}\right\rangle
,

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta_{2})\hat{a}(\eta_{1})\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta_{2})\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\lvert\eta_{2}\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{N}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.57)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\eta_{2}}_{\text{replace $\phi_{j}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}},\qquad

(2.58)

\displaystyle\hat{a}(\eta_{1})\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta_{2})\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\hat{a}(\eta_{1})\left\lvert\eta_{2}\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\eta_{2}\right\rangle\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}+\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j}\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\lvert\eta_{2}\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{N}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.59)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\eta_{2}\right\rangle\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}+\mathfrak{p}\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\eta_{2}}_{\text{replace $\phi_{j}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.60)

By defining
[\hat{A},\hat{B}]_{-\mathfrak{p}}:=\hat{A}\hat{B}-\mathfrak{p}\hat{B}\hat{A}
, we have:

\displaystyle\left[\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta_{1}),\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta_{2})\right]_{-\mathfrak{p}}=\left[\hat{a}(\eta_{1}),\hat{a}(\eta_{2})\right]_{-\mathfrak{p}}=0,\hskip 8.0pt\left[\hat{a}(\eta_{1}),\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\eta_{2})\right]_{-\mathfrak{p}}=\left\langle\eta_{1}\middle|\eta_{2}\right\rangle.

(2.61)

So for bosons (
\mathfrak{p}=1
) and
orthonormal

\{\eta_{i}\}_{i}
, they are the standard lowering and raising operators.
From Eq. (
2.58
), we also have for
orthonormal

\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}
,

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\phi_{j})\hat{a}(\phi_{j})\left\lvert\phi_{i_{1}}\cdots\phi_{i_{N}}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\sum_{k\in[N]}\delta_{ji_{k}}\lvert\phi_{i_{1}}\cdots\underbrace{\phi_{j}}_{\text{replace $\phi_{i_{k}}$}}\cdots\phi_{i_{n}}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\sum_{k\in[N]}\delta_{ji_{k}}\lvert\phi_{i_{1}}\cdots\phi_{i_{n}}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=n_{j}\left\lvert\phi_{i_{1}}\cdots\phi_{i_{N}}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.62)

counts the number of particles in
\left\lvert\phi_{i_{1}}\cdots\phi_{i_{N}}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}
occupying state
\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rangle
.

2.2.4
Second quantization in Fock space

For a general one-particle operator
\hat{O}_{\cdot}
, define its extension
\hat{O}
to
N
particles as:

\hat{O}\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rangle:=\sum_{k\in[N]}\hat{O}_{k}\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rangle,

where
\hat{O}_{k}
acts on particle
\mathrm{x}_{k}
(Eq. (
2.2
)).
Its application on a Fock state is:

\displaystyle\hat{O}\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{k\in[N]}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\left\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}\right\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\hat{O}_{\cdot}\left\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{k}}\right\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\left\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}\right\rangle

(2.63)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{k\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\underbrace{\hat{O}_{\cdot}\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rangle}_{k\text{-th}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\lvert\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N-1}}\rangle

(2.64)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\sum_{k\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{k\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}}[\phi_{1},\cdots,\hat{O}_{\cdot}\phi_{j},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,k,\ldots,\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}_{N-1}}

(2.65)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}[\phi_{1},\cdots,\hat{O}_{\cdot}\phi_{j},\cdots,\phi_{N}]_{\bm{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\pi}_{N}}

(2.66)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[N]}\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots(\hat{O}_{\cdot}\phi_{j})\cdots\phi_{N}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.67)

For a basis
\{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\}_{\alpha}
of
\mathscr{H}
, we can construct a one-particle operator:

\displaystyle\hat{A}^{(\alpha\alpha^{\prime})}_{\cdot}:=\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert,\quad\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{H}.

(2.68)

Its extension acting on a
\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{p},N}
state is given by:

\displaystyle\hat{A}^{(\alpha\alpha^{\prime})}\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\alpha}_{\text{replace $\phi_{j}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.69)

Compared with Eq. (
2.58
), we know:

\displaystyle\hat{A}^{(\alpha\alpha^{\prime})}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime}).

(2.70)

If
\{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\}_{\alpha}
is complete, then these operators
\{\hat{A}^{(\alpha\alpha^{\prime})}_{\cdot}\}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
also form a complete basis of one-particle-state operators,
so for any one-particle operator
\hat{O}_{\cdot}
, we have
\hat{O}_{\cdot}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\langle\alpha|\hat{O}_{\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert
.
So for its extension
\hat{O}
,

\displaystyle\hat{O}\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[N]}\Big\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\Big\lparen\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\langle\alpha|\hat{O}_{\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\Big\rparen\cdots\phi_{N}\Big\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\langle\alpha|\hat{O}_{\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\Big\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\alpha}_{\text{replace $\phi_{j}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\Big\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.71)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\langle\alpha|\hat{O}_{\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime})\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}},

(2.72)

which means:

\displaystyle\hat{O}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\langle\alpha|\hat{O}_{\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\hat{A}^{(\alpha\alpha^{\prime})}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\langle\alpha|\hat{O}_{\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime}).

(2.73)

Similarly, for a two-particle operator
\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}
, we have

\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\left\lvert\alpha\beta\right\rangle\langle\alpha\beta|\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\right\rvert
, and its extension

\hat{W}\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rangle:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\neq k}\hat{W}_{jk}\left\lvert\psi(\mathrm{x}_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{x}_{N})\right\rangle
(Eq. (
2.2
)). So,

\displaystyle\hat{W}\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\neq k}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\left\langle\beta^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle\langle\alpha\beta|\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rangle\big\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\alpha}_{\text{replace $\phi_{j}$}}\cdots\underbrace{\beta}_{\text{replace $\phi_{k}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}},

(2.74)

which holds since
\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\phi_{k}\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,{\alpha^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{1}){\beta^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})=\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\left\langle\beta^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle
.
On the other hand,

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\beta)\hat{a}(\beta^{\prime})\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime})\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\beta)\hat{a}(\beta^{\prime})\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.75)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\sum_{k\in[N]\setminus\{j\}}\left\langle\beta^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\big\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\underbrace{\beta}_{\text{replace $\phi_{k}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.76)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\mathfrak{p}^{j-1}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\left\langle\beta^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle\big\lvert\alpha\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\underbrace{\beta}_{\text{replace $\phi_{k}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.77)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\left\langle\beta^{\prime}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle\big\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\alpha}_{\text{replace $\phi_{j}$}}\cdots\underbrace{\beta}_{\text{replace $\phi_{k}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}},

(2.78)

so we have:

\displaystyle\hat{W}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\langle\alpha\beta|\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rangle\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\beta)\hat{a}(\beta^{\prime})\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime}).

(2.79)

For an
N
-particle Hamiltonian

\hat{H}=\hat{O}+\hat{W}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{O}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{W}^{\alpha\beta}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\beta)\hat{a}(\beta^{\prime})\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime})

where
\mathbf{O}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}:=\langle\alpha|\hat{O}_{\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle
and
\mathbf{W}^{\alpha\beta}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}:=\langle\alpha\beta|\hat{W}_{\cdot\cdot}|\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rangle
, we have:

\displaystyle\langle\psi|\hat{H}|\psi\rangle=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{O}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{W}^{\alpha\beta}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}_{\alpha\beta},

(2.80)

where
\mathbf{P}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\langle\psi|\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime})|\psi\rangle

and
\mathbf{P}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}_{\alpha\beta}:=\langle\psi|\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\beta)\hat{a}(\beta^{\prime})\hat{a}(\alpha^{\prime})|\psi\rangle

are the
one- and two-particle reduced density matrix (1- and 2-RDM)
of the
N
-particle wavefunction
\psi
.
7
7

7

They are the analogues of
\langle\alpha^{\prime}|P^{(1)}|\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|P^{(2)}|\alpha\beta\rangle
.

This is the RDM-form of the Hamiltonian under the
second quantization
formulation.
Particularly, for
\left\lvert\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\right\rangle=\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\middle|\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\hat{a}^{\dagger}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\hat{a}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\hat{a}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\middle|\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}\right\rangle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\sum_{j\neq k}\left\langle\mathrm{x}_{1}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathrm{x}_{2}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle\big\langle\phi_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1}}_{\text{replace $\phi_{j}$}}\cdots\underbrace{\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2}}_{\text{replace $\phi_{k}$}}\cdots\phi_{N}\big|\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}

(2.81)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}}\prod_{i\notin\{j,k\}}\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}\right\rangle^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{j}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{k}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})

(2.82)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\sum_{j^{\prime}\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j^{\prime}-j}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}}}\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\prod_{i\notin\{j,k\}}\langle\phi_{i}|\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{i-\mathbbl{1}_{i>j}}}\rangle^{*}\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{k-\mathbbl{1}_{k>j}}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})

(2.83)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\sum_{j^{\prime}\in[N]}\mathfrak{p}^{j^{\prime}-j}\!\!\!\!\sum_{k^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}}\!\!\mathfrak{p}^{(k^{\prime}-\mathbbl{1}_{k^{\prime}>j^{\prime}})-(k-\mathbbl{1}_{k>j})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{[N]\setminus\{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}}}\!\!\!\!\mathfrak{p}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}}\!\!\prod_{i\notin\{j,k\}}\langle\phi_{i}|\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime\prime}_{i-\mathbbl{1}_{i>j}-\mathbbl{1}_{i>k}}}\rangle^{*}\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})

(2.84)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq k}\sum_{j^{\prime}\neq k^{\prime}}\mathfrak{p}^{j-k+\mathbbl{1}_{k>j}}\mathfrak{p}^{j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}+\mathbbl{1}_{k^{\prime}>j^{\prime}}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k^{\prime}}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\left\langle\cdots\phi_{\neg j^{\prime}}\cdots\phi_{\neg k^{\prime}}\cdots\middle|\cdots\phi_{\neg j}\cdots\phi_{\neg k}\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}}

(2.85)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle P_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})

(2.86)

is the usual 2-RDM of
\psi_{\mathfrak{p}}
under the coordinate representation.
(Note in (*),
k^{\prime}-\mathbbl{1}_{k^{\prime}>j^{\prime}}
represents the position of value
k^{\prime}
in the ordered sequence
[1,\cdots,j^{\prime}-1,j^{\prime}+1,\cdots,N]
of
\bm{\pi}^{\prime}
, and
k-\mathbbl{1}_{k>j}
is the position at which
\bm{\pi}^{\prime}
is to be replaced with value
k^{\prime}
.)

2.3
Coulomb Many-Body System

2.3.1
Hamiltonian

Figure 2:
Atomic units (from
[
11
]
).

For a molecule system, under the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, the Hamiltonian for its
N
electrons given the charges and positions of the
N_{\textnormal{A}}
nuclei
\{(Z_{A},\vec{r}_{A})\}_{A\in[N_{\textnormal{A}}]}
is (adopting the atomic units; see Fig.
2
):

\displaystyle\hat{H}=\underbrace{\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}}_{=:\hat{H}^{(1)}}+\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}=\sum_{i\in[N]}\Big\lparen\hat{T}_{i}+{\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}}_{i}\Big\rparen+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}{\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}}_{ij},

(2.87)

where the terms correspond to the extensions of
\hat{T}_{i}\psi(\mathbf{x})=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi(\mathbf{x})
,

{\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}}_{i}\psi(\mathbf{x})=V_{\textnormal{Ne}}(\vec{r}_{i})\psi(\mathbf{x})=-\sum_{A\in[N_{\textnormal{A}}]}\frac{Z_{A}}{r_{iA}}\psi(\mathbf{x})
where
r_{iA}:=\left\lVert\vec{r}_{i}-\vec{r}_{A}\right\rVert
, and

{\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}}_{ij}\psi(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\psi(\mathbf{x})
where
r_{ij}:=\left\lVert\vec{r}_{i}-\vec{r}_{j}\right\rVert
.
From Eqs. (
2.6
,
2.7
,
2.8
)
, the mean energy is:

\displaystyle E[\psi]=\langle\psi|\hat{H}|\psi\rangle={}

\displaystyle-\frac{N}{2}\langle\psi|\nabla^{2}_{1}|\psi\rangle+N\langle\psi|{\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}}_{1}|\psi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}N(N-1)\langle\psi|1/r_{12}|\psi\rangle

(2.88)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2}\tr(\nabla^{2}P^{(1)}_{[\psi]})}_{T[P^{(1)}]}+\underbrace{\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi]}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]}_{E_{\textnormal{Ne}}[\rho]}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{(2)}_{[\psi]}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})}[1/r_{12}]}_{E_{\textnormal{ee}}[\rho^{(2)}]}.

(2.89)

Symbolically,

\displaystyle E[P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}]={}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{1^{\prime}}\nabla^{2}_{1=1^{\prime}}P^{1}_{1^{\prime}}+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{1}}[{V_{\textnormal{Ne}}}_{1}]+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{12}}[1/r_{12}],\text{where}

(2.90)

\displaystyle P^{1}_{1^{\prime}}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N-1}\sum\nolimits_{2^{\prime}}P^{12^{\prime}}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}},\quad\rho_{12}=P^{12}_{12},\quad\rho_{1}=\frac{1}{N-1}\sum\nolimits_{2^{\prime}}\rho_{12^{\prime}}

(2.91)

can all be expressed using the 2-RDM.
Nevertheless, the proper set of 2-RDMs is hard to describe and only increasingly better necessary conditions and sufficient conditions are known.

Note that all contractions/reductions
\left\langle\cdot\middle|\cdot\middle|\cdot\right\rangle
,
\tr(\cdot)
,
\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\cdot]
also sum over spins.
As the operators do not contain/operate on spins, the same equations hold for the spacial counterparts of
\rho
,
\rho^{(2)}
,
P^{(1)}
, and
P^{(2)}
that sum over spins beforehand.

By Property (1) of the spin-independent exchange-correlation hole,

\displaystyle E_{\textnormal{ee}}[\rho^{(2)}]={}

\displaystyle J[\rho]+E_{\textnormal{XC}}[\rho;h_{\textnormal{XC}}],\quad\text{where}

(2.92)

\displaystyle J[\rho]:={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\frac{\rho(\vec{r}_{1})\rho(\vec{r}_{2})}{r_{12}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[V_{\textnormal{J}}]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}[1/r_{12}],\text{ where }V_{\textnormal{J}}(\vec{r}):=\mathbb{E}_{\rho(\vec{r}^{\prime})}[1/\left\lVert\vec{r}-\vec{r}^{\prime}\right\rVert],

(2.93)

\displaystyle E_{\textnormal{XC}}[\rho;h_{\textnormal{XC}}]:={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\frac{\rho(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2})-\rho(\vec{r}_{1})\rho(\vec{r}_{2})}{r_{12}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\frac{\rho(\vec{r}_{1})h_{\textnormal{XC}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})}{r_{12}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{1}{h_{\textnormal{XC}}}_{21}}[1/r_{12}].

(2.94)

Here, the Hartree energy
J[\rho]
(may also be denoted as
E_{\textnormal{H}}
,
E_{\textnormal{Hart}}
) represents the
classical
electrostatic/Coulomb energy of a charge distribution with itself.
It treats
\rho
as a charge density but ignores its origin from correlated, distributed electrons.
It assumes the element forming the charge density
\rho
is infinitely divisible so any infinitesimal bulk of
\rho
can interact with other parts of
\rho
.
But this is not true since the charge of an electron cannot be divided and the Coulomb energy from the interaction of charges from the same electron should not be counted.
So there is an unphysical
self-interaction
error in
J[\rho]
.
Particularly, if
\rho
arises from just one electron distributed in space,
E_{\textnormal{ee}}
should be zero while obviously
J[\rho]
is not.
The exchange-correlation energy
E_{\textnormal{XC}}[\rho;h_{\textnormal{XC}}]
handles the
non-classical
portion,
i.e.
, the effects of self-interaction, exchange (antisymmetry), and Coulomb correlations.

2.3.2
Cusp condition

Figure 3:
Ground state wavefunction of two antiparallel electrons in
\mathrm{H}_{2}
(located at
-1
and
+1
) when fixing
x_{2}=0
(from
[
19
]
).

Origin: Kato, 1957
[
10
]
.

Laplacian in spherical coordinates

Let indices
i,j
traverse over
\{x,y,z\}
, and
\alpha,\beta
traverse over
\{r,\theta,\phi\}
.

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x=r\sin\theta\cos\phi,\\
y=r\sin\theta\sin\phi,\\
z=r\cos\theta,\end{cases}\;\left\lparen\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{i}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\alpha}}\right\rparen_{i\alpha}=\begin{pmatrix}\sin\theta\cos\phi&r\cos\theta\cos\phi&-r\sin\theta\sin\phi\\
\sin\theta\sin\phi&r\cos\theta\sin\phi&r\sin\theta\cos\phi\\
\cos\theta&-r\sin\theta&0\end{pmatrix},

(2.95)

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=g_{ij}\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{i}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{j}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\beta}}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{i}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{i}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\beta}},\;(g_{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&r^{2}&0\\
0&0&r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\end{pmatrix},\;\sqrt{\left\lvert G\right\rvert}(g^{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}r^{2}\sin\theta&0&0\\
0&\sin\theta&0\\
0&0&1/\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}.

(2.96)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}(\sqrt{\left\lvert G\right\rvert}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta})/\sqrt{\left\lvert G\right\rvert}=\partial_{r}(r^{2}\sin\theta\partial_{r})/(r^{2}\sin\theta)+\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})/(r^{2}\sin\theta)+\partial_{\phi}((1/\sin\theta)\partial_{\phi})/(r^{2}\sin\theta)

(2.97)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}(r^{2}\partial_{r})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left\lparen\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\phi}^{2}\right\rparen.

(2.98)

In
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if denoting
\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}
as the Laplacian on the
(d-1)
-dimensional unit sphere, we have:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}=\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}(r^{d-1}\partial_{r})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}.

(2.99)

Nuclear cusp condition

Consider the
N
-electron Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{i}+\sum_{i\in[N]}{\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}}_{i}+\sum_{i<j}{\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}}_{ij}
(Eq. (
2.87
)).
For a solution
\psi
to the Schrödinger equation
\hat{H}\psi=E\psi
, the local energy

\displaystyle\epsilon(\vec{\bm{r}}):=\frac{(\hat{H}\psi)(\vec{\bm{r}})}{\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\frac{\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})}{\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{A^{\prime}\in N_{\textnormal{A}}}\frac{Z_{A^{\prime}}}{r_{iA^{\prime}}}+\sum_{i<j}\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\equiv E

(2.100)

is a finite constant.
Note that the wavefunction
\psi
is continuous everywhere (hence finite everywhere), and is continuously differentiable wherever the potential is finite and may have discontinuous derivatives elsewhere.
So for a configuration
\vec{\bm{r}}=(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2},\cdots,\vec{r}_{N})
where
\vec{r}_{2},\cdots,\vec{r}_{N}
makes all of
1/r_{iA^{\prime}}
and
1/r_{ij}
finite while
\vec{r}_{1}\to\vec{r}_{A}
,
all terms in
\epsilon(\vec{\bm{r}})
are finite except
-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\nabla^{2}_{1}\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})}{\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})}
and
-\frac{Z_{A}}{r_{1A}}
.
To make
\epsilon(\vec{\bm{r}})
still a finite constant
E
,

\displaystyle\frac{\nabla^{2}_{1}\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})}{\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})}+\frac{2Z_{A}}{r_{1A}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\frac{\partial_{r}(r^{d-1}\partial_{r}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N}))}{\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})}{\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})}+\frac{2Z_{A}}{r}

(2.101)

should remain finite, where we denoted
r_{1A}
using
r
, and used the spherical coordinates
(r,\vec{\theta})
for
\vec{r}_{1}
(
\vec{\theta}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
).
Since
\psi
is finite as
r\to 0
, it is equivalent to that
\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}(r^{d-1}\partial_{r}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N}))+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})+\frac{2Z_{A}}{r}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})
is finite.
Since
\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})
is continuously differentiable in
\vec{\theta}
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}\,\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\psi(\vec{\theta},\cdots)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}\,\nabla_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\psi(\vec{\theta},\cdots)=\int_{\partial\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{d}\vec{n}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\psi(\vec{\theta},\cdots)

(2.102)

due to Stokes’ theorem, which is zero since
\partial\mathbb{S}^{d-1}=\emptyset
.
Hence, after taking the spherical average
\frac{1}{\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}\cdots
, it suffices that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}(r^{d-1}\partial_{r}\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N}))+\frac{2Z_{A}}{r}\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N})=\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N})+\frac{d-1}{r}\partial_{r}\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N})+\frac{2Z_{A}}{r}\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N})

(2.103)

is finite as
r\to 0
.
If
\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N})
is finite as
r\to 0
(
d\geqslant 2
), we then have:

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\log\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N})\big|_{r=0^{+}}=-\frac{2}{d-1}Z_{A}.

(2.104)

When
d=3
, this is
\partial_{r}\log\bar{\psi}(r,\vec{r}_{2:N})\big|_{r=0^{+}}=-Z_{A}
.

Schrödinger equation in the center-of-mass frame

Let indices
i,j
traverse over
\{x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},x_{2},y_{2},z_{2}\}
, and
\alpha,\beta
traverse over
\{x_{\textnormal{d}},y_{\textnormal{d}},z_{\textnormal{d}},x_{\textnormal{c}},y_{\textnormal{c}},z_{\textnormal{c}}\}
.

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}}=\vec{r}_{2}-\vec{r}_{1},\\
\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}=\frac{m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\vec{r}_{1}+\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\vec{r}_{2},\end{cases}\;\begin{cases}\vec{r}_{1}=-\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}}+\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\\
\vec{r}_{2}=\frac{m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}}+\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\end{cases}\;\left\lparen\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{i}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\alpha}}\right\rparen_{i\alpha}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}\\
\frac{m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix},\;\left\lvert\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{i}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\alpha}}\right\rvert_{i\alpha}=1.

(2.105)

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=g_{ij}\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{i}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\vec{r}^{j}}{\partial\vec{r}^{\beta}}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\mathbf{I}&\frac{m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}\mathbf{I}&0\\
0&m_{2}\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}\\
\frac{m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mu\mathbf{I}&0\\
0&(m_{1}+m_{2})\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix},\;\mu:=\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}.

(2.106)

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}^{2}

\displaystyle=g^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}=\frac{1}{m_{1}}\nabla^{2}_{1}+\frac{1}{m_{2}}\nabla^{2}_{2}=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}=\frac{1}{\mu}\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{d}}+\frac{1}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{c}}.

(2.107)

\displaystyle\hat{T}

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{d}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{c}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=3}^{N}\nabla^{2}_{i}\;\;\;\;\text{($i$ here indexes electrons again)}.

(2.108)

Electronic cusp condition

First consider two
antiparallel-spin
electrons (no Pauli exclusion), numbered
1
and
2
, so that
\psi
remains finite and nonzero as
r_{12}\to 0
while all other
1/r_{ij}
and
1/r_{iA^{\prime}}
are finite.
The local energy (Eq. (
2.100
)) is finite iff
-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{2}_{1}+\nabla^{2}_{2})\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})/\psi(\vec{\bm{r}})+1/r_{12}
is.
Using Eq. (
2.108
), it requires that
(-\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{d}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{c}})\psi(\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})/\psi(\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})+1/r_{\textnormal{d}}
is finite as
r_{\textnormal{d}}\to 0
,
where we denoted
r_{12}
as
r_{\textnormal{d}}
and used the center-of-mass coordinate system.
Since
\psi
is finite (may be
0
) as
r_{\textnormal{d}}\to 0
, it suffices that
(-\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{d}}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{c}})\psi(\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})+\psi(\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})/r_{\textnormal{d}}
is finite (may be
0
).
As the local kinetic energy of the center-of-mass / two-electron-system as a whole,
-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{c}}\psi
should remain finite as the relative distance
r_{\textnormal{d}}\to 0
.
So
-\nabla^{2}_{\textnormal{d}}\psi(\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})+\psi(\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})/r_{\textnormal{d}}
should be finite.
Following a similar derivation for the nuclear cusp condition, we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{r_{\textnormal{d}}}\log\bar{\psi}(r_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})\big|_{r_{\textnormal{d}}=0^{+}}=\frac{1}{d-1},

(2.109)

for
d\geqslant 2
, where
\bar{\psi}(r_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})
is the spherical average of
\psi(\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})
over the direction of
\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}}
.
When
d=3
, this is
\partial_{r_{\textnormal{d}}}\log\bar{\psi}(r_{\textnormal{d}},\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3:N})\big|_{r_{\textnormal{d}}=0^{+}}=\frac{1}{2}
.
For a general expression, the cusp condition can be written as:

\displaystyle\partial_{r_{ij}}\log\bar{\psi}(r_{ij},{\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}}_{ij},\vec{r}_{\neg\{i,j\}})\big|_{r_{ij}=0^{+}}=\frac{2\mu_{ij}Z_{i}Z_{j}}{d-1}.

(2.110)

Fig.
3
shows the cusp of the two-electron (with antiparallel spin) wavefunction in
\mathrm{H}_{2}
molecule.
It shows two observations.

(i)
If Pauli exclusion principle is not explicitly enforced, the Coulomb repulsion between two electrons cannot enforce the wavefunction to go zero at coincidence, although the potential is infinitely large there.

(ii)
Although electron coincidence is locally less probable, the region that the cusp condition plays a role may not be “minor” / “less important”, since the neighbourhood of coincidence may take a high probability.

A concise reasoning

The cusp condition also applies to
inter-electron Coulomb interaction
with more subtlety.
When
\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}
(for
i\neq j
), leaving only the dominating potential, from Eq. (
2.87
) the Schrödinger equation becomes
-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{k}\psi+\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\psi=E\psi
.

(I)
If
s_{i}=s_{j}
, then Pauli exclusion principle applies and
\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{i},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=-\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{j},\vec{r}_{i},\dots)
.
So
\lim_{\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}}\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{i},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=\lim_{\vec{r}_{j}\to\vec{r}_{i}}\nabla^{2}_{j}\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{j},\vec{r}_{i},\dots)=-\lim_{\vec{r}_{j}\to\vec{r}_{i}}\nabla^{2}_{j}\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{i},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=-\lim_{\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}}\nabla^{2}_{j}\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{i},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)

(need some continuity assumptions in the last equality?),
and
\lim_{\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}}\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{i},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=0
hence
\lim_{\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}}\nabla^{2}_{k}\psi=0
for
k\neq i,j
.
Taking the limit, the equation becomes
\lim_{\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}}\psi/r_{ij}=0
,
which means that the Coulomb repulsion makes the antisymmetric wavefunction
\psi
for
parallel spin
decay faster than linear at the boundary.

(II)
If
s_{i}\neq s_{j}
,
i.e.
for
antiparallel spins
, there is no constraint on the spacial wavefunction
a priori
(the two electrons have different spins hence distinguishable).
Apart from
\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi
, the term
\nabla^{2}_{j}\psi
may also be infinite in the limit since
\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}
leads to the same singularity as
\vec{r}_{j}\to\vec{r}_{i}
.
Other kinetic energy terms for
k\neq i,j
as well as
E\psi
remain finite in the limit.
So leaving only dominating terms, the equation becomes
-\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi-\nabla^{2}_{j}\psi+\frac{2}{r_{ij}}\psi=0
, or
(r_{ij}\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi-\psi)+(r_{ji}\nabla^{2}_{j}\psi-\psi)=0
.
Following the same deduction above for
r_{iA}\to 0
, we have
\frac{\partial\bar{\psi}^{(j)}}{\partial r_{ij}}(\dots,r_{ij},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)+\frac{\partial\bar{\psi}^{(i)}}{\partial r_{ji}}(\dots,\vec{r}_{i},r_{ji},\dots)-\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{i},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=0
, where we made explicit the center for the spherical average.
Since when
\vec{r}_{i}\to\vec{r}_{j}
,
\bar{\psi}^{(j)}(\dots,r_{ij}=0,\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=\bar{\psi}^{(i)}(\dots,\vec{r}_{i}=\vec{r}_{j},r_{ji}=0,\dots)
, the first two terms are equal (needs more investigation).
So we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{ij}\to 0}\left\lparen\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{ij}}-\frac{1}{2}\right\rparen\bar{\psi}(\dots,r_{ij},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=\lim_{r_{ij}\to 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{ij}}\bar{\psi}(\dots,r_{ij},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)-\frac{1}{2}\psi(\dots,\vec{r}_{i}=\vec{r}_{j},\vec{r}_{j},\dots)=0,

(2.111)

which also holds when taking
i
as the center of spherical average (
i.e.
, swapping
i
and
j
).

A more detailed and precise derivation

Ref.:
[
4
]
.
Denote
\vec{r}
in place of the
\vec{r}_{\textnormal{d}}
above as the relative displacement of two electrons in the center-of-mass system.
In the notation, omit other coordinates,
\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}},\vec{r}_{3},\cdots,\vec{r}_{N}
, since they does not change or cause divergence as
r\to 0
.
If we let the wavefunction be in the form:

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{r})=e^{-u(r)}f(\vec{r}),

(2.112)

where
f(\vec{r})
is
smooth
and
antisymmetric
(
e.g.
, a combination of some Slater determinants),
then by noting
\nabla r=\nabla\left\lVert\vec{r}\right\rVert=\hat{r}
and
\nabla\hat{r}^{\top}=(I-\hat{r}\hat{r}^{\top})/r
where
\hat{r}:=\vec{r}/r
is the unit vector in the direction of
\vec{r}
which is a function only of
\vec{\theta}
, we have:

\displaystyle\nabla\psi(\vec{r})={}

\displaystyle-u^{\prime}(r)e^{-u(r)}f(\vec{r})\hat{r}+e^{-u(r)}\nabla f(\vec{r}),

(2.113)

\displaystyle\nabla\nabla^{\top}\psi(\vec{r})={}

\displaystyle\big(-u^{\prime\prime}(r)+u^{\prime}(r)^{2}\big)e^{-u(r)}f(\vec{r})\hat{r}\hat{r}^{\top}-u^{\prime}(r)e^{-u(r)}\big(\hat{r}\nabla f(\vec{r})^{\top}+\nabla f(\vec{r})\hat{r}^{\top}\big)-\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}e^{-u(r)}f(\vec{r})(I-\hat{r}\hat{r}^{\top})+e^{-u(r)}\nabla\nabla^{\top}f(\vec{r})

(2.114)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big(-u^{\prime\prime}(r)+u^{\prime}(r)^{2}+\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}\Big)e^{-u(r)}f(\vec{r})\hat{r}\hat{r}^{\top}-u^{\prime}(r)e^{-u(r)}\Big(\hat{r}\nabla f(\vec{r})^{\top}+\nabla f(\vec{r})\hat{r}^{\top}+\frac{f(\vec{r})}{r}I\Big)+e^{-u(r)}\nabla\nabla^{\top}f(\vec{r}),

(2.115)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\psi(\vec{r})={}

\displaystyle\Big(-u^{\prime\prime}(r)+u^{\prime}(r)^{2}+\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}\Big)e^{-u(r)}f(\vec{r})-2u^{\prime}(r)e^{-u(r)}\nabla f(\vec{r})\cdot\hat{r}-3u^{\prime}(r)e^{-u(r)}\frac{f(\vec{r})}{r}+e^{-u(r)}\nabla^{2}f(\vec{r})

(2.116)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big(-u^{\prime\prime}(r)+u^{\prime}(r)^{2}-2\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}\Big)e^{-u(r)}f(\vec{r})-2u^{\prime}(r)e^{-u(r)}\nabla f(\vec{r})\cdot\hat{r}+e^{-u(r)}\nabla^{2}f(\vec{r}).

(2.117)

Electronic cusp condition is derived from the finiteness of:

\displaystyle-\frac{\nabla^{2}\psi(\vec{r})}{\psi(\vec{r})}+\frac{1}{r}=u^{\prime\prime}(r)-u^{\prime}(r)^{2}+2\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}+2u^{\prime}(r)\frac{\nabla f(\vec{r})\cdot\hat{r}}{f(\vec{r})}-\frac{\nabla^{2}f(\vec{r})}{f(\vec{r})}+\frac{1}{r}

(2.118)

(reduced mass
\mu=\frac{1}{2}
is used) when
r\to 0
.
For antiparallel-spin electrons,
f(\vec{0})\neq 0
in general, so we require

2\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}+\frac{1}{r}=0
to avoid
\frac{1}{r}
divergence.
This leads to
u^{\prime}(0)=-\frac{1}{2}
.
For parallel-spin electrons,

\displaystyle f(\vec{r})=\nabla f(\vec{0})\cdot\vec{r}+O(r^{3})=(\nabla f(\vec{0})\cdot\hat{r})r+O(r^{3}).

(2.119)

This indicates
\nabla^{2}f(\vec{r})=O(r)
, so
\frac{\nabla^{2}f(\vec{r})}{f(\vec{r})}=O(1)
hence finite. So we require

2\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}+2\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{r}+\frac{1}{r}=0
to avoid
\frac{1}{r}
divergence.
This leads to
u^{\prime}(0)=-\frac{1}{4}
.

Using this result, we can expand
\psi
near
\vec{0}
.
For antiparallel-spin electrons,
\nabla\psi(\vec{0})=\big(\frac{1}{2}\hat{r}+\frac{\nabla f(\vec{0})}{f(\vec{0})}\big)e^{-u(0)}f(\vec{0})
, so:

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{r})=\psi(\vec{0})\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}r+\frac{\nabla f(\vec{0})\cdot\hat{r}}{f(\vec{0})}r\Big)+O(r^{2}).

(2.120)

Taking spherical average gives
\bar{\psi}(r)=\psi(\vec{0})\big(1+\frac{1}{2}r\big)+O(r^{2})
, which agrees with Eq. (
2.109
).
For parallel-spin electrons,

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{r})=e^{-u(0)}\nabla f(\vec{0})\cdot\vec{r}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-u(0)}(\nabla f(\vec{0})\cdot\vec{r})r+O(r^{3})=e^{-u(0)}(\nabla f(\vec{0})\cdot\hat{r})\Big(r+\frac{1}{4}r^{2}\Big)+O(r^{3}).

(2.121)

Taking spherical average gives
\bar{\psi}(r)=O(r^{3})
.
This also agrees with Eq. (
2.109
), which indicates
\bar{\psi}^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Note it also holds that
\bar{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
.

Eqs. (
2.120
,
2.121
)
can also be seen from another way.
Let
\psi(\vec{r})
keep
-\frac{\nabla^{2}\psi(\vec{r})}{\psi(\vec{r})}+\frac{1}{r}
finite.
Separate the variable
\vec{r}=(r,\vec{\theta})
in
\psi
and expand the
\vec{\theta}
factor with spherical harmonics:

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{r})=\psi(r,\vec{\theta})=\sum_{l=l_{0}}^{\infty}\psi_{l}(r,\vec{\theta}),\qquad\psi_{l}(r,\vec{\theta}):=R_{l}(r)\sum_{m=-l}^{l}b_{lm}Y_{lm}(\vec{\theta}).

(2.122)

If we impose the
r\to 0
finiteness of local energy for each
\psi_{l}
, by noting Eq. (
2.99
) and that
\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}=\hat{L}^{2}
is the squared angular momentum operator, this means the
r\to 0
finiteness of:

\displaystyle-\frac{\nabla^{2}\psi_{l}(r,\vec{\theta})}{\psi_{l}(r,\vec{\theta})}+\frac{1}{r}=-\frac{R_{l}^{\prime\prime}}{R_{l}}-\frac{2R_{l}^{\prime}}{rR_{l}}-\frac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r}.

(2.123)

This is similar to the radial equation for hydrogen atoms.
To keep it finite as
r\to 0
, we have:

\displaystyle R_{l}(r)=C_{l}r^{l}\Big(1+\frac{Z_{i}Z_{j}\mu_{ij}}{l+1}r+O(r^{2})\Big)=C_{l}r^{l}\Big(1+\frac{1}{2(l+1)}r+O(r^{2})\Big).

(2.124)

Since
\hat{L}^{2}=\hat{S}^{2}+\hat{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{orbit}}
, the total angular momentum is contributed from the spins and the spacial (orbital) motion,
we know that
l\geqslant s
, so
l_{0}=s
, so for antiparallel-spin electrons,
l_{0}=0
(singlet state), and for parallel-spin electrons,
l_{0}=1
(triplet state).
So for antiparallel-spin electrons, noting
Y_{00}(\vec{\theta})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}
is constant, we have:

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{r})=C_{0}\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}r\Big)+C_{1}r\sum\nolimits_{m=-1}^{1}b_{1m}Y_{1m}(\vec{\theta})+O(r^{2}).

(2.125)

This agrees with Eq. (
2.120
).
For the latter, not only does the summation index
l
need to be larger than
l_{0}=1
.
Since two parallel-spin electrons are indistinguishable, the wavefunction needs to be antisymmetric, so only odd
l
are allowed, whose corresponding
Y_{lm}(\vec{\theta})
has odd parity.
So the wavefunction near coincidence is:

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{r})=C_{1}\Big(r+\frac{1}{4}r^{2}\Big)\sum\nolimits_{m=-1}^{1}b_{1m}Y_{1m}(\vec{\theta})+O(r^{3}).

(2.126)

This agrees with Eq. (
2.121
).
Note that for antiparallel-spin electrons, the wavefunction is not symmetric in general, except
e.g.
, there are only these two electrons.
For Slater-Jastrow ansätz, the two coordinates are input into two different Slater determinants, so switching their values (even if they are close) does not yield the same (close) determinant values. So
\nabla f(\vec{0})\neq\vec{0}
.
This is why Eqs. (
2.120
,
2.125
)
has odd-parity terms.
In all, the electronic cusp condition is:

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}^{\prime}(r=0)\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{1}{2}\bar{\psi}(r=0),

(2.127)

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{r})\propto\begin{cases}1+\frac{1}{2}r+(\vec{a}\cdot\hat{r})r+O(r^{2}),&\text{antiparallel-spin, Eqs.~(\ref{eqn:cusp-wavefn-electron-antipara}, \ref{eqn:cusp-wavefn-electron-sph-harmonic-antipara})
},\\
(\vec{a}\cdot\hat{r})\Big(r+\frac{1}{4}r^{2}\Big)+O(r^{3}),&\text{parallel-spin, Eqs.~(\ref{eqn:cusp-wavefn-electron-para}, \ref{eqn:cusp-wavefn-electron-sph-harmonic-para})
}.\end{cases}

(2.128)

Cusp condition for density

“At any position of an atom
\vec{r}_{A}
,
\rho(\vec{r})
exhibits a maximum with a finite value, while its gradient there has a discontinuity and a cusp result”
[
11
]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\log\bar{\rho}(r)\big|_{r=0^{+}}=-2Z_{A},

(2.129)

where
r
is the radial coordinate of the spherical coordinates centered at
\vec{r}_{A}
, and
\bar{\rho}(r)
is the spherical average of
\rho(\vec{r})=\rho(r,\vec{\theta})
,
i.e.

\bar{\rho}(r)=\frac{1}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}\,\rho(r,\vec{\theta})
.
This can be seen from Eq. (
2.104
):

\partial_{r}\bar{\rho}(r)\big|_{r=0^{+}}=\frac{N}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\partial_{r}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2:N}\left\lvert\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})\right\rvert^{2}\big|_{r=0^{+}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{2N}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2:N}\psi\partial_{r}\psi^{*}\big|_{r=0^{+}}=
8
8

8

This deduction requires the cusp condition to hold in any direction:
\partial_{r}\log\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})\big|_{r=0^{+}}=-Z_{A}
for any
\vec{\theta}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
This is somehow not obvious from the above condition Eq. (
2.101
):

\partial_{r}^{2}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})+\frac{2Z_{A}}{r}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})
is finite,
since if
\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})=O(r)
, then it is
2\partial_{r}\psi+2Z_{A}\psi+\frac{1}{r}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi=0
that should vanish.
Nevertheless, it can be argued that as
r\to 0
, the dominating potential
\frac{1}{r}
is spherically symmetric, so is
\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})
as
r\to 0
, hence
\frac{1}{r}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi\to 0
as
r\to 0
.

-2Z_{A}\frac{N}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2:N}\left\lvert\psi(r,\vec{\theta},\vec{r}_{2:N})\right\rvert^{2}=-2Z_{A}\bar{\rho}(0)
.
Since the Hamiltonian does not explicitly depend on spin, the deduction applies to
\psi^{\bm{s}}(\cdots)
and
\rho^{s}(\vec{r})
as well.
(Even when the nucleus
A
also has a half-integer spin, it is anyway a different, distinguishable particle from electron
i
, so Pauli exclusion principle does not apply.)

For one-electron density
\rho(\vec{r})
, only the nuclear cusp is relevant.
For pair density and holes, “since no two electrons of parallel spin can occupy the same point in space, this cusp condition occurs only for electrons of antiparallel spins”
[
11
]
,
so it leads to a cusp condition for the Coulomb hole
h_{\textnormal{C}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1})
.
For this, we have

\partial_{r_{12}}\rho_{s,\neg s}(\vec{r}_{1}=\vec{r}_{2},\vec{r}_{2}):=\partial_{r_{12}}\bar{\rho}_{s,\neg s}({\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}}_{12},r_{12}=0):=\partial_{r_{12}}\frac{1}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{12}\,\rho_{s,\neg s}({\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}}_{12},r_{12}=0,\vec{\theta}_{12})=\frac{N(N-1)}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\partial_{r_{12}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{12}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{3:N}\left\lvert\psi({\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}}_{12},r_{12}=0,\vec{\theta}_{12},\vec{r}_{3:N})\right\rvert^{2}=\frac{N(N-1)}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{12}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{3:N}\big(\psi^{*}\partial_{r_{12}}\psi+\psi\partial_{r_{12}}\psi^{*}\big)=
8

\frac{N(N-1)}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{12}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{3:N}\left\lvert\psi({\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}}_{12},r_{12}=0,\vec{\theta}_{12},\vec{r}_{3:N})\right\rvert^{2}=\frac{1}{\Omega(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{12}\rho_{s,\neg s}({\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}}_{12},r_{12}=0,\vec{\theta}_{12})=\bar{\rho}_{s,\neg s}({\vec{r}_{\textnormal{c}}}_{12},r_{12}=0)=\rho_{s,\neg s}(\vec{r}_{1}=\vec{r}_{2},\vec{r}_{2})
.

2.3.3
Large-distance asymptotic behavior

When walking far away from all nuclei
r\to\infty
, the inter-electron potential can be neglected, so the Schrödinger equation from Eq. (
2.87
) becomes
\sum_{i\in[N]}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}_{i}\psi-\frac{Z}{r_{i}}\psi=E\psi
, where
Z:=\sum_{A\in[N_{\textnormal{A}}]}Z_{A}
is the total charge.
The Hamiltonian in this case is separable, so we consider the one-electron equation,
-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\psi-\frac{Z}{r}\psi=-E_{\textnormal{I}}\psi
,
where
E_{\textnormal{I}}
is the exact first ionization energy of the system.
Again, leveraging the Laplacian spherical decomposition and taking the spherical average, we have

-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\psi}}{{\mathrm{d}r}^{2}}-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\psi}}{\mathrm{d}r}-\frac{Z}{r}\bar{\psi}=-E_{\textnormal{I}}\bar{\psi}
,
which gives
\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\bar{\psi}}{{\mathrm{d}r}^{2}}=E_{\textnormal{I}}\bar{\psi}
in the limit
r\to\infty
, whose solution is (omitting the oscillating solution and only take the decaying solution):

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}(r)\propto\exp\{-\sqrt{2E_{\textnormal{I}}}\,r\},\hskip 8.0pt\rho(\vec{r})\propto\exp\{-2\sqrt{2E_{\textnormal{I}}}\,r\}.

(2.130)

3
Hartree-Fock Methods

3.1
Wavefunction Assumptions

A1: Linearly-dependent spacial wavefunction

\displaystyle\psi^{\downarrow,s_{2}\ldots s_{N}}(\vec{r}_{1},\cdots,\vec{r}_{N})=S\psi^{\uparrow,s_{2}\ldots s_{N}}(\vec{r}_{1},\cdots,\vec{r}_{N}),

(3.1)

where
S
is a constant (spin-flipping coefficient).
Roughly means that position
\vec{r}_{1}
change does not change the probability of taking a spin value; which roughly means there is no spin-orbit coupling.

This assumption means
\psi^{s_{1}\ldots\downarrow\ldots}(\vec{r}_{1},\cdots,\vec{r}_{i},\cdots)=-\psi^{\downarrow\ldots s_{1}\ldots}(\vec{r}_{i},\cdots,\vec{r}_{1},\cdots)=-S\psi^{\uparrow\ldots s_{1}\ldots}(\vec{r}_{i},\cdots,\vec{r}_{1},\cdots)=S\psi^{s_{1}\ldots\uparrow\ldots}(\vec{r}_{1},\cdots,\vec{r}_{i},\cdots)
,
so the
spin-flipping coefficient
S
is the same for any electron
.

Moreover,
\psi^{s_{1}s_{2}}=\psi^{s_{2}s_{1}}
if
s_{1}=s_{2}
.
If not, suppose
s_{1}=\downarrow
,
s_{2}=\uparrow
, then
\psi^{s_{1}s_{2}}=S\psi^{\uparrow\uparrow}=\psi^{\uparrow\downarrow}=\psi^{s_{2}s_{1}}
.
So the
wavefunction is invariant against spin permutation
.

Explanation:

From
Wikipedia
,
“
\psi(\vec{r},s,t)=\psi(\vec{r},t)\xi(s,t)
. The tensor product factorization is only possible if the orbital and spin angular momenta of the particle are separable in the Hamiltonian operator underlying the system’s dynamics (in other words, the Hamiltonian can be split into the sum of orbital and spin terms[28]). The time dependence can be placed in either factor, and time evolution of each can be studied separately. The factorization is not possible for those interactions where an external field or any space-dependent quantity couples to the spin; examples include a particle in a magnetic field, and spin–orbit coupling.”
If only eigenstates are considered, in that case,
\psi(\vec{r},s)=\psi(\vec{r})\xi(s)
, so
\psi(\vec{r},\downarrow)=\psi(\vec{r})\xi(\uparrow)\frac{\xi(\downarrow)}{\xi(\uparrow)}=S\psi(\vec{r},\uparrow)
.

3.1.1
A2: Slater determinant (Hartree-Fock wavefunction) (UHF)

It is the Fock state Eq. (
2.15
) for fermions.
Results in Sec.
2.2
hold for subscript “S” (for “Slater”) as for
\mathfrak{p}=-1
.

\displaystyle\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x}):=\left\lvert\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{N}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{p}=-1}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\det\left[\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{j})\right]_{ij}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N}).

(3.2)

Further assume the
orthonormality
of the spin-orbitals
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
(or just called orbitals;
i.e.
, one-electron wavefunctions):

\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x})^{*}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x})=\delta_{ij}
.

The determinant makes
\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})
exactly antisymmetric,
so it naturally
handles the Fermi/exchange correlation,
i.e.

\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x})=0
, for any pair of electrons with
parallel
spins
(see Eq. (
3.11
) below).
The
orthonormality
of
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
makes
\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})
normalized:

\left\langle\psi_{\textnormal{S}}\middle|\psi_{\textnormal{S}}\right\rangle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\det\left[\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle\right]_{ij}=1.

Note that for each spin-orbital
\phi_{k}
,
\phi_{k}^{\uparrow}(\vec{r})
and
\phi_{k}^{\downarrow}(\vec{r})
are two different spacial orbitals.
So for open-shell systems this assumption does not guarantee being an eigenfunction of the total spin operator
\hat{\vec{S}}^{2}
(it is still eigenfunction of
\hat{S}_{z}
) [
source
].
This corresponds to the
Unrestricted Hartree-Fock method (UHF)

[
5
]
.

Densities, correlations and holes.

The 1- and 2-RDMs and the density
and the pair density
9
9

9

Direct derivation using
orthonormality
:

\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2}):=N(N-1)\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{3}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\left\lvert\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{N(N-1)}{N!}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{3}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime},\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})

(3.3)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{N(N-1)}{N!}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime},\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{3}\bm{\pi}_{3}}\cdots\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N}\bm{\pi}_{N}}

(3.4)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{N(N-1)}{N!}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\left\lparen\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})-\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{2}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rparen

(3.5)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\neq l}\left\lparen\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})-\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rparen=\sum_{k,l}\left\lparen\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})-\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rparen

(3.6)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\sum_{l\in[N]}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})-\sum_{l\in[N]}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\sum_{k\in[N]}\big(\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big)^{*}=\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})-\Big\lvert\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\Big\rvert^{2}.

(3.7)

under the Slater determinant with
orthonormal

\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are:

\displaystyle P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime})\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}),

(3.8)

\displaystyle P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\big(\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})-\phi_{k}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})\phi_{j}^{*}(\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})\big)\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1})P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})-P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{2})P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2};\mathrm{x}^{\prime}_{1}),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x})\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N]}\left\lvert\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x})\right\rvert^{2},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})-\Big\lvert\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\Big\rvert^{2}.

(3.11)

We see indeed
\rho_{\textnormal{S}}^{s,s}(\vec{r},\vec{r})=0
for any parallel-spin electron pair.

For the pair density of an antiparallel-spin electron pair,
assume A2.2:

for any
k\in[N]
, either
\phi_{k}^{\uparrow}\equiv 0
or
\phi_{k}^{\downarrow}\equiv 0
;
or equivalently, for any
k\in[N]
,
\phi_{k}^{s}(\vec{r}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}^{\neg s}(\vec{r}_{2})\equiv 0

(
e.g.
, under A2.1 below; but A2.1 can be made more general: only impose the orthonormality of spin basis but not the specific spin basis).
Then we have:

\displaystyle\rho_{\textnormal{S}}^{s,\neg s}(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2})\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\rho_{\textnormal{S}}^{s}(\vec{r}_{1})\rho_{\textnormal{S}}^{\neg s}(\vec{r}_{2}),

(3.12)

which indicates two antiparallel-spin electrons distribute
independently
(unnecessarily implies they do not interact).

By definition, the exchange-correlation hole function is:

\displaystyle h_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathrm{x}_{2}|\mathrm{x}_{1}):=\frac{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})}{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})}-\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})}\Big\lvert\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\Big\rvert^{2},

(3.13)

and the correlation factor is
f(\mathrm{x}_{1},\mathrm{x}_{2})=-\frac{\left\lvert\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}}{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})}
. The spin-independent hole (total hole) is:

\displaystyle h_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1}):=\frac{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{2})}{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r}_{1})}-\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r}_{2})=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r}_{1})}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\Big\lvert\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}^{s_{1}}(\vec{r}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}^{s_{2}}(\vec{r}_{2})\Big\rvert^{2}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle h_{\textnormal{X}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\vec{r}_{2}|\vec{r}_{1}):=-\frac{1}{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r}_{1})}\sum_{s}\Big\lvert\sum_{k\in[N]}\phi_{k}^{s}(\vec{r}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}^{s}(\vec{r}_{2})\Big\rvert^{2}.

(3.15)

Under A2.2, the summation is over parallel spins only, so the HF total hole is seen as composed of the exchange hole only,
i.e.

h_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}=h_{\textnormal{X}}^{\textnormal{HF}}
.
It well handles self-interaction and exchange correlation (it cancels the self-interaction term and is the full consequence of antisymmetry (determinant)), so it is the exact exchange hole in HF.
This may also be a definition of the exchange hole (see Sec.
2.1.2
, trial (III), though don’t know the correspondence of a general wavefunction to the orbitals).
But the Coulomb hole is zero, which should be there based on a qualitative analysis.
So HF with A2.2 is seen to have missed the Coulomb correlation (and the kinetic energy error).

Explanation:

The Slater determinant
\psi_{\textnormal{S}}
is the form of the exact ground-state solution (except for some degenerate cases
[
14
]
) of a system of
N

non-interacting
fermions (no direct/physical inter-particle interaction like the Coulomb repulsion) moving in some maybe-effective, one-particle potential
V_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r})
.
The Hamiltonian is:

\displaystyle\hat{H}=\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}=\sum_{i\in[N]}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}_{i}+{\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}}_{i},\quad\text{where }{\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}}_{i}\psi(\mathbf{x}):=V_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r}_{i})\psi(\mathbf{x}).

(3.16)

3.1.2
A2.1: Open- and Closed-Shell Systems (R(O)HF)

In many cases, two antiparallel-spin electrons can be seen to occupy the same spacial orbital.
So we can pair two spin-orbitals and make them share the same spacial part:

\displaystyle\{\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x})\}_{i=1}^{N}=\{\varphi_{a}(\vec{r})\delta^{\uparrow}_{s}\}_{a=1}^{N^{\uparrow}}\cup\{\varphi_{b}(\vec{r})\delta^{\downarrow}_{s}\}_{b=1}^{N^{\downarrow}},

(3.17)

where
N^{\uparrow}+N^{\downarrow}=N
,
\{\varphi_{a}(\vec{r})\}_{a=1}^{N_{\textnormal{sp}}}
with
N_{\textnormal{sp}}:=\max\{N^{\uparrow},N^{\downarrow}\}
is a set of spacial orbitals,
and the spin basis
\{\delta^{\uparrow}_{s},\delta^{\downarrow}_{s}\}
are
orthonormal
:

\langle\delta^{\alpha}_{\cdot}|\delta^{\beta}_{\cdot}\rangle=\sum_{s}\delta^{\alpha}_{s}\delta^{\beta}_{s}=\delta_{\alpha\beta}
.
The spin-orbital
orthonormality
then indicates the
orthonormality
of spacial orbitals:
\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\varphi^{*}_{a}(\vec{r})\varphi_{a^{\prime}}(\vec{r})=\delta_{aa^{\prime}},\forall a,a^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{sp}}]
.
Note that for any spin-orbital
\phi_{i}=\varphi_{a}\delta^{\uparrow}_{\cdot}
or
=\varphi_{b}\delta^{\downarrow}_{\cdot}
, either
\phi_{i}^{\uparrow}\equiv 0
or
\phi_{i}^{\downarrow}\equiv 0
, so A2.2 holds.

Closed-shell
system:
N^{\uparrow}=N^{\downarrow}=N_{\textnormal{sp}}
.
This corresponds to the
Restricted Hartree-Fock method (RHF)
.
Otherwise, it is called an
Open-shell
system: there is unpaired electron (orbital), and the multiplicity / spin polarization number (the number of unpaired electrons, the number of singly-occupied orbitals)
N_{\textnormal{sp}}-N_{\textnormal{pr}}
is not zero, where
N_{\textnormal{pr}}:=\min\{N^{\uparrow},N^{\downarrow}\}
is the pair number of paired electrons (number of doubly-occupied orbitals).
This corresponds to the
Restricted Open-shell Hartree-Fock method (ROHF)

[
17
]
.
“In contrast to UHF, the R(O)HF wavefunction Eq. (
3.17
) is a satisfactory eigenfunction of the total spin operator
\hat{\vec{S}}^{2}
(no spin contamination).” [
Wikipedia
]

10
10

10

Words based on
[
11
]
:
Closed-shell systems correspond to restricted Hartree-Fock (RHF).
The open-shell Hartree-Fock method is called unrestricted Hartree-Fock (UHF).
The major disadvantage of UHF is that it is not an eigenfunction of the total spin operator
\hat{\vec{S}}^{2}
.
A variant is the restricted open-shell HF scheme (ROHF), but it is much more complicated than UHF (ROHF wavefunction is a limited linear combination of a few determinants), though it does not meet the problem of not being an eigenfunction of
\hat{\vec{S}}^{2}
.

The wavefunction then becomes:

\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{1}^{s_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}_{1}})\cdots\phi_{N}^{s_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}_{N}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\bigg\lparen\prod_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}_{a}})\delta^{\uparrow}_{s_{\bm{\pi}_{a}}}\bigg\rparen\bigg\lparen\prod_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}_{b+N^{\uparrow}}})\delta^{\downarrow}_{s_{\bm{\pi}_{b+N^{\uparrow}}}}\bigg\rparen

(3.18)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\star}}}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N^{\uparrow}}}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N^{\downarrow}}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}\bigg\lparen\prod_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}^{\star}_{\bm{\pi}_{a}}})\bigg\rparen\bigg\lparen\prod_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}^{\star}_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{b}+N^{\uparrow}}})\bigg\rparen

(3.19)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\star}}}{\sqrt{N!}}\det[\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}^{\star}_{a^{\prime}}})]_{a,a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\det[\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{\bm{\pi}^{\star}_{b^{\prime}+N^{\uparrow}}})]_{b,b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}

(3.20)

if
\#\{i\in[N]\mid s_{i}=\uparrow\}=N^{\uparrow}
in which case
\bm{\pi}^{\star}
is a permutation that makes
s_{\bm{\pi}^{\star}_{a}}=\uparrow
for all
a\in[N^{\uparrow}]
,
otherwise
\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})=0
.
This indicates that either
\psi_{\textnormal{S}}^{\uparrow,s_{2}\ldots s_{N}}
or
\psi_{\textnormal{S}}^{\downarrow,s_{2}\ldots s_{N}}
must be zero.
If it is the former, then A1 holds with
S=0
; otherwise A1 does not hold (or holds in another direction with
S=0
).
In either case, swapping different spins changes the sign.

Explanation:

This is adopted when the Hamiltonian does not involve spins.
Note however that it is
not
the case when spin-orbit coupling, or spin-spin interaction, are considered,
e.g.
in Hund’s rule.
Due to the explanation for A1, in this case, the wavefunction can be separated into a spacial and a spin part:
\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x})=\phi_{i}(\vec{r})\xi_{i}(s)
.
Moreover, due to the spacial-spin disentanglement, the wavefunction basis can be taken as the Cartesian product of spacial orbitals and spin basis,

\{\varphi_{a}(\vec{r})\}_{a=1}^{N_{\textnormal{sp}}}\times\{\delta^{\uparrow}_{s},\delta^{\downarrow}_{s}\}
.
This is possible since a spacial orbital does not have a preference on the spin due to the disentanglement (no spin-orbit or cross-orbit spin-spin interaction),
and any orbit-dependent, non-eigenstate spin basis
\{\varphi_{a}(\vec{r})\xi_{a,1}(s),\varphi_{a}(\vec{r})\xi_{a,2}(s)\}
can be expressed under this orbit-free, eigenstate basis.

3.2
Energy Expressions

3.2.1
Energy under Slater determinant A2 (UHF)

The electronic Hamiltonian of a molecular system is given by Eq. (
2.87
).
From Eqs. (
2.89
,
2.92
)
, we have the energy under the Slater determinant with
orthonormal

\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}
:
11
11

11

Direct derivation using
orthonormality
:

\langle\psi_{\textnormal{S}}|\hat{H}^{(1)}_{i}|\psi_{\textnormal{S}}\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{N}\,\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})^{*}\hat{H}^{(1)}_{i}\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime},\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{1}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\hat{H}^{(1)}_{i}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})

(3.21)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime},\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\pi}_{1}}\cdots\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{\neg i}\bm{\pi}_{\neg i}}\cdots\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N}\bm{\pi}_{N}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\hat{H}^{(1)}_{i}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})

(3.22)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\hat{H}^{(1)}_{i}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\,\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\hat{H}^{(1)}_{i}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\langle\phi_{k}|\hat{H}^{(1)}|\phi_{k}\rangle,

(3.23)

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{\textnormal{S}}|(1/r_{ij})|\psi_{\textnormal{S}}\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\,\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})^{*}(1/r_{ij})\psi_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathbf{x})

(3.24)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{N}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime},\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})\cdots\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{N}}(\mathrm{x}_{N})

(3.25)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{j}\sum_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime},\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}}(-1)^{\bm{\pi}}\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\pi}_{1}}\cdots\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{\neg i}\bm{\pi}_{\neg i}}\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{\neg j}\bm{\pi}_{\neg j}}\cdots\delta_{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{N}\bm{\pi}_{N}}

(3.26)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{j}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\mathrm{\Pi}_{N}}\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\left\lparen\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})-\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{\bm{\pi}_{j}}(\mathrm{x}_{j})\right\rparen

(3.27)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{j}\sum_{k\neq l}\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\left\lparen\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{j})-\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{j})\right\rparen

(3.28)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{j}\sum_{k,l\in[N]}\frac{1}{r_{ij}}\left\lparen\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{j})-\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{i})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{i})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{j})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{j})\right\rparen

(3.29)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{k,l\in[N]}\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}(I^{ss^{\prime}}_{kl|kl}-I^{ss^{\prime}}_{lk|kl})=\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{k,l\in[N]}(I_{kl|kl}-I_{lk|kl})=\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{k,l\in[N]}(J_{kl}-K_{kl}).

(3.30)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptE[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]=T[P^{(1)}_{\textnormal{S}}]+E_{\textnormal{Ne}}[\rho_{\textnormal{S}}]+J[\rho_{\textnormal{S}}]+E_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}],\quad\text{where}

(3.31)

\displaystyle T[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[N]}\left\langle\phi_{k}\middle|\nabla^{2}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[N]}\sum_{s}\left\langle\phi_{k}^{s}\middle|\nabla^{2}\middle|\phi_{k}^{s}\right\rangle,

(3.32)

\displaystyle E_{\textnormal{Ne}}[\rho_{\textnormal{S}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\sum_{k\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x})\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}(\vec{r})]=\sum_{k\in[N]}\sum_{s}\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\phi_{k}^{s}(\vec{r})\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}(\vec{r})],

(3.33)

\displaystyle J[\rho_{\textnormal{S}}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})}\Big[\frac{1}{r_{12}}\Big]\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l\in[N]}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\left\lvert\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}}{r_{12}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l}J_{kl},

(3.34)

\displaystyle E_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\lvert\sum_{k}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\rvert^{2}}{r_{12}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})}{r_{12}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l}K_{kl}.

(3.35)

In sum,

\displaystyle E[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in[N]}\langle\phi_{k}|\nabla^{2}|\phi_{k}\rangle+\sum_{k\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\phi_{k}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l\in[N]}J_{kl}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l\in[N]}K_{kl}.\hskip 36.98866pt\text{(UHF)}\hskip-36.98866pt

(3.36)

In the above equations, by defining
4-center-2-electron integral
:
