\displaystyle I_{ij|kl}:=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\frac{1}{r_{12}}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})=\left\langle\phi_{i}\otimes\phi_{j}\middle|\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}\middle|\phi_{k}\otimes\phi_{l}\right\rangle,

(3.37)

which by construction satisfies:

\displaystyle I_{ij|kl}=I^{*}_{kl|ij}=I_{ji|lk},

(3.38)

we have used the
Coulomb integral
:

\displaystyle J_{kl}:=I_{kl|kl}=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})}{r_{12}}=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\left\lvert\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\lvert\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\rvert^{2}}{r_{12}}

(3.39)

that describes the Coulomb interaction between a pair of electrons occupying orbitals
k
and
l
with any possible spin configurations, and the
exchange integral
:

\displaystyle K_{kl}:=I_{kl|lk}=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{1})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{l}(\mathrm{x}_{1})\phi_{k}(\mathrm{x}_{2})}{r_{12}}

(3.40)

that arises from the permutation in the Slater determinant, and comes in the form of exchanging the two electrons.
By definition,
J_{kl}=J_{lk}
,
K_{kl}=K_{lk}
.
Note in Eqs. (
3.34
,
3.35
)
,
k=l
is allowed, since the unphysical
self-interaction

J_{kk}\neq 0
is cancelled by
K_{kk}=J_{kk}
.

3.2.2
Energy under A2.1 (R(O)HF)

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}:=\{N_{\textnormal{pr}}+1,\cdots,N_{\textnormal{sp}}\}
denote the index set of unpaired electrons / singly-occupied spacial orbitals.
Under A2.1 Eq. (
3.17
), for each
\phi_{k}
, only one value of
s
is activated, so from Eqs. (
3.32
,
3.33
)
,

\displaystyle T[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\langle\varphi_{a}|-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}|\varphi_{a}\rangle+\sum_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\langle\varphi_{b}|-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}|\varphi_{b}\rangle=2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\langle\varphi_{p}|-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}|\varphi_{p}\rangle+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\langle\varphi_{u}|-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}|\varphi_{u}\rangle,

(3.41)

\displaystyle E_{\textnormal{Ne}}[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\varphi_{a}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]+\sum_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\varphi_{b}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]=2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\varphi_{p}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\varphi_{u}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}].

(3.42)

For the classical Coulomb energy (Hartree energy), from Eq. (
3.34
),

\displaystyle 2J[\rho_{\textnormal{S}}]=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}}{r_{12}}\sum_{k}\sum_{s_{1}}\left\lvert\phi_{k}^{s_{1}}(\vec{r}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{l}\sum_{s_{2}}\left\lvert\phi_{l}^{s_{2}}(\vec{r}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}

(3.43)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}}{r_{12}}\bigg\lparen\sum_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\sum_{s_{1}}\left\lvert\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{1})\delta^{\uparrow}_{s_{1}}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\sum_{s_{1}}\left\lvert\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{1})\delta^{\downarrow}_{s_{1}}\right\rvert^{2}\bigg\rparen\bigg\lparen\sum_{a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\sum_{s_{2}}\left\lvert\varphi_{a^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{2})\delta^{\uparrow}_{s_{2}}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\sum_{s_{2}}\left\lvert\varphi_{b^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{2})\delta^{\downarrow}_{s_{2}}\right\rvert^{2}\bigg\rparen

(3.44)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}}{r_{12}}\bigg\lparen\sum_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\left\lvert\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\left\lvert\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\bigg\rparen\bigg\lparen\sum_{a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\left\lvert\varphi_{a^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\left\lvert\varphi_{b^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}\bigg\rparen

(3.45)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\tilde{J}_{aa^{\prime}}+2\sum_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\sum_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\tilde{J}_{ab}+\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\tilde{J}_{bb^{\prime}}

(3.46)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 4\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\tilde{J}_{pp^{\prime}}+4\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\tilde{J}_{pu}+\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\tilde{J}_{uu^{\prime}},

(3.47)

where
\tilde{J}_{ab}:=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\varphi^{*}_{a}(\vec{r}_{1})\varphi^{*}_{b}(\vec{r}_{2})\frac{1}{r_{12}}\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{1})\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{2})=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\frac{\left\lvert\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{1})\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}}{r_{12}}
describes the Coulomb interaction between two electrons occupying spacial orbitals
a
and
b
.
By definition,
\tilde{J}_{ab}=\tilde{J}_{ba}
.

For the exchange-correlation energy, from Eq. (
3.35
),

\displaystyle-2E_{\textnormal{XC}}^{\textnormal{HF}}[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}}{r_{12}}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\sum_{k}\phi_{k}^{s_{1}}(\vec{r}_{1})^{*}\phi_{k}^{s_{2}}(\vec{r}_{2})\sum_{l}\phi_{l}^{s_{1}}(\vec{r}_{1})\phi_{l}^{s_{2}}(\vec{r}_{2})^{*}

(3.48)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}}{r_{12}}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\bigg\lparen\sum_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{1})^{*}\delta^{\uparrow*}_{s_{1}}\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{2})\delta^{\uparrow}_{s_{2}}+\sum_{b\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{1})^{*}\delta^{\downarrow*}_{s_{1}}\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{2})\delta^{\downarrow}_{s_{2}}\bigg\rparen\cdots

(3.49)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt\bigg\lparen\sum_{a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\varphi_{a^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{1})\delta^{\uparrow}_{s_{1}}\varphi_{a^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{2})^{*}\delta^{\uparrow*}_{s_{2}}+\sum_{b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\varphi_{b^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{1})\delta^{\downarrow}_{s_{1}}\varphi_{b^{\prime}}(\vec{r}_{2})^{*}\delta^{\downarrow*}_{s_{2}}\bigg\rparen

(3.50)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\tilde{I}_{aa^{\prime}|a^{\prime}a}\langle\delta^{\uparrow}|\delta^{\uparrow}\rangle\langle\delta^{\uparrow}|\delta^{\uparrow}\rangle+\sum_{a\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\sum_{b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\tilde{I}_{ab^{\prime}|b^{\prime}a}\langle\delta^{\uparrow}|\delta^{\downarrow}\rangle\langle\delta^{\downarrow}|\delta^{\uparrow}\rangle

(3.51)

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\sum_{b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\tilde{I}_{ba^{\prime}|a^{\prime}b}\langle\delta^{\downarrow}|\delta^{\uparrow}\rangle\langle\delta^{\uparrow}|\delta^{\downarrow}\rangle+\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\tilde{I}_{bb^{\prime}|b^{\prime}b}\langle\delta^{\downarrow}|\delta^{\downarrow}\rangle\langle\delta^{\downarrow}|\delta^{\downarrow}\rangle

(3.52)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\tilde{I}_{aa^{\prime}|a^{\prime}a}+\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\tilde{I}_{bb^{\prime}|b^{\prime}b}=\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in[N^{\uparrow}]}\tilde{K}_{aa^{\prime}}+\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in[N^{\downarrow}]}\tilde{K}_{bb^{\prime}}

(3.53)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\tilde{K}_{pp^{\prime}}+\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\tilde{K}_{uu^{\prime}},

(3.54)

where (*) is due to the orthonormality of the spin basis under A2.1, and

\tilde{K}_{ab}:=\tilde{I}_{ab|ba}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\varphi^{*}_{a}(\vec{r}_{1})\varphi^{*}_{b}(\vec{r}_{2})\frac{1}{r_{12}}\varphi_{b}(\vec{r}_{1})\varphi_{a}(\vec{r}_{2})
.
By definition,
\tilde{K}_{ab}=\tilde{K}_{ba}
.
Note that different from the Coulomb term, orbitals only with parallel spins contribute to the exchange term.
Finally, the energy for ROHF is:

\displaystyle E[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]={}

\displaystyle 2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\Big\lparen\langle\varphi_{p}|-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}|\varphi_{p}\rangle+\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\varphi_{p}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]\Big\rparen+\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\Big\lparen 2\tilde{J}_{pp^{\prime}}-\tilde{K}_{pp^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\cdots

(RHF)

(3.55)

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\Big\lparen\langle\varphi_{u}|-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}|\varphi_{u}\rangle+\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\varphi_{u}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]\Big\rparen+2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\tilde{J}_{pu}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\Big\lparen\tilde{J}_{uu^{\prime}}-\tilde{K}_{uu^{\prime}}\Big\rparen.

(ROHF)

(3.56)

For RHF,
N^{\uparrow}=N^{\downarrow}=N_{\textnormal{sp}}=N_{\textnormal{pr}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}=\emptyset
, so only the first line of the above equation remains.

If defining running
i\in[N]
as running
a\in[N^{\uparrow}]
and
b\in[N^{\downarrow}]
(or running
p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]

twice
and
u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}
once), then:

\displaystyle E[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[N]}\Big\lparen\langle\varphi_{i}|-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}|\varphi_{i}\rangle+\mathbb{E}_{\left\lvert\varphi_{i}\right\rvert^{2}}[V_{\textnormal{Ne}}]\Big\rparen+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j\in[N]}\tilde{J}_{ij}-\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i,j\in[N]}\tilde{K}_{ij},&\text{\phantom{O}(RHF)}\\
\frac{1}{2}\Big\lparen 2\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\tilde{K}_{pp^{\prime}}+\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\tilde{K}_{uu^{\prime}}\Big\rparen,&\text{(ROHF)}\end{cases}

(3.57)

which defers from Eq. (
3.36
) only in the
\hat{K}
term where the summation is only over parallel-spin spin-orbital pairs.

3.3
Hartree-Fock Equation

To minimize the energy Eq. (
3.36
) as a functional of the orbitals
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
subject to the
orthonormal
constraint,
taking the variation w.r.t
\phi_{i}^{*}
(complex derivative in the sense of Eq. (
0.2
), similar to the derivation from Eq. (
1.30
)) with Lagrange multiplier (
orbital energy
)
\varepsilon_{i}
for the constraint
\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x})^{*}\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x})=1
(the orthogonality is naturally satisfied if there is no degeneracy) yields the
Hartree-Fock equation
:

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\phi_{i}:={}

\displaystyle\big(\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}\big)\phi_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i},\quad\text{where}\;\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}:=\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{J}^{(j)}-\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{K}^{(j)},

(3.58)

\displaystyle\text{and}\;\hat{J}^{(j)}\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{1}):=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}}{r_{12}}\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{1}),\qquad\hat{K}^{(j)}\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{1}):=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})^{*}\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{2})}{r_{12}}\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{1}).

(3.59)

The
Fock operator

\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
involves the
Hartree-Fock potential operator

\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}
, which reforms the inter-electron interactions into an equivalent,
effective
potential that acts on each single electron (represented as its action onto each one-electron orbital).
Note that both
\hat{J}^{(j)}
and
\hat{K}^{(j)}
hence
\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}
and
\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
are operators determined by the orbitals
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
Also note that due to
\hat{K}^{(j)}
, the effective potential operator
\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}
in HF is
non-local
.
Compared with Eq. (
3.36
), the factor
\frac{1}{2}
is dropped since the index
i
appears twice in the scans of
k
and of
l
.

For the
Coulomb operator

\hat{J}^{(j)}
and the
exchange operator

\hat{K}^{(j)}
, we have
\langle\phi_{i}|\hat{J}^{(j)}|\phi_{i}\rangle=J_{ij}
,
\langle\phi_{i}|\hat{K}^{(j)}|\phi_{i}\rangle=K_{ij}
.
The term
\hat{J}^{(j)}\phi_{i}
represents the effect of the Coulomb potential from all other electrons (with all possible spins), and also from itself (
j=i
is included in the summation).
The unphysical self-interaction
\hat{J}^{(i)}\phi_{i}
is offset by
\hat{K}^{(i)}\phi_{i}
, so
the self-interaction is well-handled
in Hartree-Fock methods.
The term
\hat{K}^{(j)}\phi_{i}
for
j\neq i
accounts for the antisymmetry of wavefunction,
i.e.
correcting for the proper exchange property.
So
the exchange correlation (Fermi hole) is also well-handled
.

The operator
\sum_{j}\hat{J}^{(j)}
can also be written as the classical potential
V_{\textnormal{J}}
from the electron charge density:

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{J}^{(j)}\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{1})=:\hat{V}_{\textnormal{J}}\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{1})=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\,V_{\textnormal{J}}(\vec{r}_{1})\phi_{i}(\mathrm{x}_{1}),\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle V_{\textnormal{J}}(\vec{r}_{1}):=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\lvert\phi_{j}(\mathrm{x}_{2})\right\rvert^{2}}{r_{12}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x}_{2})}{r_{12}}=\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}_{2}\,\frac{\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r}_{2})}{r_{12}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces

Using the new operators, for
orthonormal
orbitals
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, the energy Eq. (
3.36
) can also be expressed as:

\displaystyle E[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]=\sum_{k\in[N]}\left\langle\phi_{k}\middle|\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{l\in[N]}\hat{J}^{(l)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{l\in[N]}\hat{K}^{(l)}\middle|\phi_{k}\right\rangle.

(3.60)

Using the
orthonormal
solution to the eigenvalue problem Eq. (
3.58
), the HF ground-state energy is:

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{HF}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\varepsilon_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\langle\phi_{i}|\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}|\phi_{i}\rangle=\sum_{i\in[N]}\varepsilon_{i}-\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{J}^{(j)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{K}^{(j)}\middle|\phi_{i}\right\rangle,

(3.61)

but is not
\sum_{i\in[N]}\langle\phi_{i}|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}|\phi_{i}\rangle=\sum_{i\in[N]}\varepsilon_{i}
.

Eq. (
3.58
) is a
pseudo-eigenvalue problem
, as the Fock operator
\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
itself depends on the solution
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
So an iterated procedure is used until convergence (
self-consistent field, SCF
).

3.3.1
Hartree-Fock equation for R(O)HF (under A2.1)

Taking the variation of Eq. (
3.56
) w.r.t
\varphi_{p}^{*}
and
\varphi_{u}^{*}
(see Eqs. (
0.2
,
1.30
)
) for
p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]
and
u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}
yields:

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\varphi_{p}:=\big(\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}\big)\varphi_{p}=\varepsilon_{p}\varphi_{p},

\displaystyle\;\text{where}\;\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}=\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\Big\lparen\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}2\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(p^{\prime})}+\sum\nolimits_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(u^{\prime})}\Big\rparen-\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(p^{\prime})},

(3.62)

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\varphi_{u}:=\big(\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}\big)\varphi_{u}=\varepsilon_{u}\varphi_{u},

\displaystyle\;\text{where}\;\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}=\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\Big\lparen\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}2\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(p^{\prime})}+\sum\nolimits_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(u^{\prime})}\Big\rparen-\sum\nolimits_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(u^{\prime})}.

(3.63)

For the sets of equations in the first line, the Lagrange multiplier for
\frac{\updelta}{\updelta\varphi_{p}^{*}}
is
2\varepsilon_{p}
, and the common factor of 2 is canceled from both sides.
For a more concise expression in the spirit of Eq. (
3.57
), for
a\in[N_{\textnormal{sp}}]
,

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\varphi_{a}:=\big(\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}\big)\varphi_{a}=\varepsilon_{a}\varphi_{a},\quad\text{where}

(3.64)

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}={}

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(j)}-\mathbb{I}_{\cdot\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(p^{\prime})}-\mathbb{I}_{\cdot\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\sum\nolimits_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(u^{\prime})}

(3.65)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(j)}-\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\bar{K}}^{(j)},\quad\text{where}\;\hat{\bar{K}}^{(b)}\varphi_{a}:=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{I}_{a,b\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(b)}\varphi_{a}+\mathbb{I}_{a,b\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(b)}\varphi_{a}.

(3.66)

Note that this is not the variational equation directly from Eq. (
3.57
), as the equations for
\varphi_{p},p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]
are divided by 2.
Using the
orthonormal
solution to Eq. (
3.64
), the energy can be expressed as follows by comparing
\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
in Eq. (
3.64
) with Eq. (
3.57
):

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{HF}}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\underbrace{\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\tilde{J}_{ij}}_{\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(j)}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle}+\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\underbrace{\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\tilde{K}_{pp^{\prime}}}_{\left\langle\varphi_{p}\middle|\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(p^{\prime})}\middle|\varphi_{p}\right\rangle}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\underbrace{\sum\nolimits_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\tilde{K}_{uu^{\prime}}}_{\left\langle\varphi_{u}\middle|\sum\nolimits_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(u^{\prime})}\middle|\varphi_{u}\right\rangle}

(3.67)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(j)}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\mathbb{I}_{\cdot\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(p^{\prime})}+\mathbb{I}_{\cdot\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\sum\nolimits_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(u^{\prime})}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle

(3.68)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(j)}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\bar{K}}^{(j)}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle

(3.69)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[N]}\varepsilon_{i}-\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(j)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\bar{K}}^{(j)}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle

(3.70)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\varepsilon_{p}+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\varepsilon_{u}-\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\Big\lparen 2\tilde{J}_{pp^{\prime}}-\tilde{K}_{pp^{\prime}}\Big\rparen-2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\tilde{J}_{pu}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\Big\lparen\tilde{J}_{uu^{\prime}}-\tilde{K}_{uu^{\prime}}\Big\rparen.

(3.71)

Alternatively, by comparing
\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
in Eq. (
3.64
) with Eq. (
3.69
), we have:

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{HF}}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\frac{1}{2}\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{T}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\varepsilon_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle

(3.72)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\varepsilon_{p}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\varepsilon_{u}+\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\left\langle\varphi_{p}\middle|\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}\middle|\varphi_{p}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\left\langle\varphi_{u}\middle|\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}\middle|\varphi_{u}\right\rangle.

(3.73)

Again, this is not just
\sum_{i\in[N]}\langle\varphi_{i}|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}|\varphi_{i}\rangle=\sum_{i\in[N]}\varepsilon_{i}=2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\varepsilon_{p}+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{I}_{\textnormal{upr}}}\varepsilon_{u}
.

For RHF, the equation reduces to:

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\varphi_{p}:=\big(\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}\big)\varphi_{p}=\varepsilon_{p}\varphi_{p},\quad\text{where}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{HF}}=\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\sum\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\big\lparen 2\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(p^{\prime})}-\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(p^{\prime})}\big\rparen=\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{J}}^{(j)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{\tilde{K}}^{(j)}.

For the energy, Eq. (
3.72
) simplifies to:

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{HF}}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\varepsilon_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle=\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\varepsilon_{p}+\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\langle\varphi_{p}|\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}|\varphi_{p}\rangle

(3.74)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\varepsilon_{p}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\varphi_{i}\middle|\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{J}^{(j)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{j\in[N]}\hat{K}^{(j)}\middle|\varphi_{i}\right\rangle=2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\varepsilon_{p}-\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\big\lparen 2\tilde{J}_{pp^{\prime}}-\tilde{K}_{pp^{\prime}}\big\rparen,

(3.75)

but is not
\sum_{i\in[N]}\langle\varphi_{i}|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}|\varphi_{i}\rangle=2\sum_{p\in[N_{\textnormal{pr}}]}\varepsilon_{p}
.

3.4
Roothaan Equation

Let
\{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\}_{\alpha\in[N_{\textnormal{B}}]}
be a basis set for one-electron wavefunctions.
The orbitals can then be written as:
\phi_{i}=\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle
(the matrix form takes the superscript as the row index).
The Hartree-Fock equation (any case) then becomes
\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle=\varepsilon_{i}\sum_{\beta}\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{i}\left\lvert\beta\right\rangle
.
Taking inner product with
\left\lvert\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}\bm{\upvarepsilon}:\quad\sum_{\alpha}(\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}})^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\sum_{\beta}\mathbf{S}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta}\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{i},\;\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in[N_{\textnormal{B}}],i\in[N],

(3.76)

where
(\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}})^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}|\alpha\rangle
is the Fock matrix (the Fock operator
\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
is defined in Eq. (
3.58
)),

\mathbf{S}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\alpha\rangle
is the overlap matrix,
and
\bm{\upvarepsilon}:=\Diag[\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{N}]
.
The orthonormality constraint on the orbitals translates into
\delta_{ij}=\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}{\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}}^{*}\left\langle\alpha\middle|\beta\right\rangle\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{j}
,
or
\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{I}
.

Eq. (
3.76
) is a generalized eigenvalue problem: it is equivalent to
\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}\bm{\upvarepsilon}
.
Using the orthonormality, we also have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}=\bm{\upvarepsilon},

(3.77)

so solving the problem is equivalent to diagonalizing the Fock matrix
\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
.
This gives
N_{\textnormal{B}}
eigenstates while only
N\leqslant N_{\textnormal{B}}
is needed as (occupied) orbitals, but all eigenstates need to be solved so as to collect
N
eigenstates with largest eigenvalues as the orbitals.
Other eigenstates may also be used as virtual/excited orbitals in post-HF methods.

Calculation of the Fock matrix.

First note from Eq. (
3.8
), the density matrix is

P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime})=\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle(\mathrm{x})\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i}}^{*}\left\lvert\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle(\mathrm{x}^{\prime})^{*}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}{\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i}}^{*}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}^{\prime})
,
so the density matrix under this basis is
\mathbf{P}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}{\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i}}^{*}
,
or
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}
, so that

P_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x};\mathrm{x}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{x}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\langle\mathrm{x}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}^{\prime}\rangle
.
Note that this
\mathbf{P}
corresponds to the
\mathbf{\Gamma}
(not
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
) in Eq. (
1.37
).
From Eq. (
3.10
), the density is

\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x})=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\big(\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}{\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i}}^{*}\big)\langle\mathrm{x}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}\rangle=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\langle\mathrm{x}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}\rangle=\tr\Big(\mathbf{P}\big(\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert\big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\Big)(\mathrm{x})
.
The normalization constraint is
\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\mathrm{x})=\tr(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S})=N
.
This can also be seen from Eq. (
1.38
), where
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is taken as
\mathbf{P}
, and
\hat{A}
is taken as the identity so
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\middle|\alpha\right\rangle=\mathbf{S}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}
is the overlap matrix according to Eq. (
1.36
).
Mean value of other operators can also be computed using Eq. (
1.38
).

The last two terms in
\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
in Eq. (
3.58
) lead to the Hartree matrix
\mathbf{J}
and the exchange matrix
\mathbf{K}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:={}

\displaystyle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\sum_{j}\hat{J}^{(j)}|\alpha\rangle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\beta}_{\beta^{\prime}}\langle\mathrm{x}_{2}|\beta\rangle\langle\beta^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}_{2}\rangle}{r_{12}}\left\langle\mathrm{x}_{1}\middle|\alpha\right\rangle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}_{1}\rangle=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\beta}_{\beta^{\prime}}I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\alpha\beta}=\tr(\mathbf{P}\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}),

(3.78)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:={}

\displaystyle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\sum_{j}\hat{K}^{(j)}|\alpha\rangle\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\sum_{j\in[N]}\frac{\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}{\mathbf{C}^{\beta^{\prime}}_{j}}^{*}\langle\beta^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}_{2}\rangle\langle\mathrm{x}_{2}|\alpha\rangle}{r_{12}}\sum_{\beta}\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{j}\left\langle\mathrm{x}_{1}\middle|\beta\right\rangle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}_{1}\rangle

(3.79)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\beta}_{\beta^{\prime}}\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}_{2}\,\frac{\langle\beta^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}_{2}\rangle\langle\mathrm{x}_{2}|\alpha\rangle}{r_{12}}\left\langle\mathrm{x}_{1}\middle|\beta\right\rangle\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\mathrm{x}_{1}\rangle=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\beta}_{\beta^{\prime}}I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\beta\alpha}=\tr(\mathbf{P}\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}),

(3.80)

where
(\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta}:=I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\alpha\beta}
, and
(\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta}:=I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\beta\alpha}
.
Note that
\mathbf{J}
and
\mathbf{K}
depend on
\mathbf{P}
, which is taken as the previous-step
\mathbf{P}
while solving Eq. (
3.76
) for the current step.
Constructing
\mathbf{J}
and
\mathbf{K}
requires 4-center-2-electron integrals and incurs an
O(N^{4})
complexity, but density fitting techniques could make it
O(N^{3})
.

The Fock matrix can then be expressed as:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}})^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{T}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}+(\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}+\tr(\mathbf{P}\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})-\tr(\mathbf{P}\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})=\mathbf{T}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}+(\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{J}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{K}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha},

(3.81)

where
\mathbf{T}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\tr(\hat{T}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert)=-\frac{1}{2}\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\nabla^{2}|\alpha\rangle
, and

(\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\tr(\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert)=\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}|\alpha\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\alpha^{\prime}(\mathrm{x})^{*}\alpha(\mathrm{x})V_{\textnormal{Ne}}(\vec{r})
.
Using the density matrix, Eq. (
3.77
) indicates
\tr(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}})=\tr(\bm{\upvarepsilon})
,
so the energy of the system can be estimated by

E[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]=\frac{1}{2}\tr(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}})+\frac{1}{2}\tr\big(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})\big)
.
This is the common expression for UHF and R(O)HF, Eqs. (
3.61
,
3.72
,
3.74
)
.
For R(O)HF, just use the appropriate index set for summing over
\hat{K}^{(j)}
.

Orbital orthonormality.

Obviously the overlap matrix
\mathbf{S}
and the density matrix
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}
are Hermitian.
Note that the Fock operator hence the Fock matrix is also Hermitian,
(\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}})^{\dagger}=\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
:
from Eq. (
3.58
), we know that
\hat{T}
is Hermitian, also are
\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}
and
\hat{J}^{(j)}
since they are real-valued, multiplicative operators (sometimes also called local operators);
for
\sum_{j}\hat{K}^{(j)}
, from Eq. (
3.80
) and noting Eq. (
3.38
),
\mathbf{K}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}=\langle\alpha^{\prime}|\sum_{j}\hat{K}^{(j)}|\alpha\rangle=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}^{\beta}_{\beta^{\prime}}I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\beta\alpha}=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{P}^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta})^{*}I_{\beta\alpha|\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}^{*}=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{P}^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta})^{*}I_{\alpha\beta|\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{*}=\langle\alpha|\sum_{j}\hat{K}^{(j)}|\alpha^{\prime}\rangle^{*}=(\mathbf{K}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{*}
.

We now show that
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{j}=0
for
i\neq j
if given non-degeneracy
\varepsilon_{i}\neq\varepsilon_{j}
, so to realize the orbital orthonormality requirement
\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{I}
, we only need to normalize each orbital coefficient:
\mathbf{C}_{i}\leftarrow\mathbf{C}_{i}/\sqrt{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{i}}
.

From the Roothaan equation Eq. (
3.76
)
\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{i}
, we have

\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{j}=\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{j}}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}_{j}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{j}}(\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}_{i})^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{j}=\frac{\varepsilon_{i}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{j}
(since
\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
is Hermitian),
so
\big\lparen 1-\frac{\varepsilon_{i}}{\varepsilon_{j}}\big\rparen\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{j}=0
, which implies
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{j}=0
.

Alternatively, as
\mathbf{S}
is positive definite and Hermitian, let
\mathbf{M}
be a non-singular matrix s.t.
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}
.
Then the Roothaan equation Eq. (
3.76
)
\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{i}
implies
\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{i}
,
which is
\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{M}^{-\dagger}\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{i}
,
i.e.
,
\{(\varepsilon_{i},\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{i})\}_{i}
are eigenpairs of
\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{M}^{-\dagger}
.
Since
(\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{M}^{-\dagger})^{\dagger}=\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{M}^{-\dagger}
is Hermitian, once
\varepsilon_{i}\neq\varepsilon_{j}
, the corresponding eigenvectors are orthogonal:

0=(\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{i})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{j})=\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{j}=\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{j}
.
Note that although the equation can be reformulated as
\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\mathbf{C}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}
, this unnecessarily implies
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{C}_{j}=0
, since
\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}
is not Hermitian.

Density matrix optimization.

For large systems, it is more efficient to only finding the lowest
N
eigenvectors instead of all of them.
Density matrix (referring to the 1-RDM) optimization is one approach.
Noting Eqs. (
2.89
,
3.60
)
and Eq. (
1.38
) and the above argument, the variational energy in terms of density matrix
\mathbf{P}
is:

\displaystyle E(\mathbf{P})={}

\displaystyle\tr(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{T})+\tr(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})+\frac{1}{2}\tr\Big(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{P})\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\tr\Big(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{P})\Big)

(3.82)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\tr\Big(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\tr\Big(\mathbf{P}\tr\big(\mathbf{P}(\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}-\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})\big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\Big).

(3.83)

Here we explicitly write the dependency of
\mathbf{J}
,
\mathbf{K}
on
\mathbf{P}
.
It can also be derived by plugging the basis expansion of orbitals
\phi_{i}=\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle
into Eq. (
2.89
) (also noting Eq. (
3.11
)).

Note that
\tr(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B})=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathbf{B}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{A}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})^{*}\mathbf{B}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}(\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)})^{*}\bar{\mathbf{B}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}=\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{\dagger}\bar{\mathbf{B}}
,
where
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}:=\mathbf{A}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}
(sim.
\bar{\mathbf{B}}
) is the flattened vector.
So the first term
\tr\big(\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})\big)=\tr\big((\mathbf{P}^{\dagger})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}})\big)=\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\dagger}(\bar{\mathbf{T}}+\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne}})

if we let
\bar{\mathbf{T}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}:=\mathbf{T}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}
(sim.
\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne}}
),
and
\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}:=(\mathbf{P}^{\dagger})^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}
, which is also
\mathbf{P}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}=(\mathbf{P}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{*}
since
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}^{\dagger}
.

Note also that
(\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{\dagger})_{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}=(\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)})^{*}=(\mathbf{A}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})^{*}=(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
,
which indicates if
\tr(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{B})=\bar{\mathbf{L}}\bar{\mathbf{B}}
, then
\bar{\mathbf{L}}_{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}:=\mathbf{L}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
So the second term
\tr\Big(\mathbf{P}\tr\big(\mathbf{P}(\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}-\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})\big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\Big)=\tr\Big((\mathbf{P}^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\tr\big((\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}-\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})\mathbf{P}\big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\Big)=\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\dagger}(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}})\bar{\mathbf{P}}
,
if we let
\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}_{(\beta\beta^{\prime})}:=(\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta}=I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\alpha\beta}
,
and
\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}_{(\beta\beta^{\prime})}:=(\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta}=I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\beta\alpha}
.
Note that
\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}^{(\beta\beta^{\prime})}_{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}=I_{\beta\alpha|\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}=I_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}|\beta\alpha}^{*}=I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\alpha\beta}^{*}=(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}_{(\beta\beta^{\prime})})^{*}
,
and
\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}^{(\beta\beta^{\prime})}_{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}=I_{\beta\alpha|\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}=I_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}|\beta\alpha}^{*}=(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}_{(\beta\beta^{\prime})})^{*}
,
so both
\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}
and
\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
are Hermitian matrices.
Eq. (
3.83
) then becomes:

\displaystyle E(\bar{\mathbf{P}})=\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\dagger}(\bar{\mathbf{T}}+\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne}})+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\dagger}(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}})\bar{\mathbf{P}},

(3.84)

which is a quadratic form of
\bar{\mathbf{P}}
.
This directly gives the gradient (see Sec.
0.3
) w.r.t the density matrix:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}E(\bar{\mathbf{P}})\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{T}}+\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}})\bar{\mathbf{P}},\quad\text{or}

(3.85)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P})={}

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\Big(\tr\Big((\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}-\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})\mathbf{P}\Big)\Big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{P})-\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{P})=\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{P}),

(3.86)

where the last equality is due to Eq. (
3.81
)

In the optimization, orbital orthonormality
\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{I}
is still required, which is necessary for the derivation of the energy expression Eq. (
3.83
).
The constraint indicates
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}
, which is
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}
, meaning that the density matrix
\mathbf{P}
is
idempotent
.
(Note that
\mathbf{P}
is likely degenerate, so this does not mean
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{I}
or
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{S}^{-1}
.)
Introduce Lagrange multipliers
\mathbf{\Lambda}
for
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{P}
, which is Hermitian since
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{S}
are.
The gradient of the constraint term is:

\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}\tr(\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{P}))=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{P}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}^{\dagger}+\mathbf{S}^{\dagger}\mathbf{P}^{\dagger}\mathbf{\Lambda}-\mathbf{\Lambda}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{\Lambda}-\mathbf{\Lambda}
.
The optimized
\mathbf{P}^{\star}
then satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star})={}

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{P}^{\star})-\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{P}^{\star})=\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\tr\big\lparen\mathbf{P}^{\star}(\bm{\mathsf{J}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}-\bm{\mathsf{K}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})\big\rparen^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}

(3.87)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}-\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{P}^{\star}.

(3.88)

Multiplying both sides with
\mathbf{P}^{\star}
yields:

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star})\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{\star},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{P}^{\star}.

(3.89)

Taking the trace, the l.h.s becomes
\tr({\mathbf{P}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star}))
, the r.h.s becomes
\tr(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{\star})=\tr(\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star})=\tr(\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star})=\tr({\mathbf{P}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star})
. So:

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\bar{\mathbf{f}}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star})={\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\bar{\mathbf{T}}+{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}}^{\dagger}(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}})\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}={\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\bar{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\star},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{P}^{\star}.

(3.90)

The normalization constraint
\tr(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S})=N
can be formulated as
{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\bar{\mathbf{S}}=N
.
If ignoring the constraint
\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{P}^{\star}
and only imposing the constraint
{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\bar{\mathbf{S}}=N
with Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
, taking the gradient w.r.t
\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}
yields:

\displaystyle 2(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}})\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\star}+\lambda\bar{\mathbf{S}}-\bar{\mathbf{T}}-\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne}},\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}=\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}})^{-1}(\bar{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\star}+\lambda\bar{\mathbf{S}}-\bar{\mathbf{T}}-\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne}}),

(3.91)

if assuming
\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
to be non-singular.

3.5
Energy Gradient and Relation Among the Methods

Given a conformation
\mathbf{R}:=\{(Z_{A},\vec{r}_{A})\}_{A}
and an atomic basis expansion of orbitals
\phi_{i}=\phi_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{C}_{i}}:=\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle_{\mathbf{R}}
, there are two equivalent ways from
E_{\mathbf{R}}[\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}]
Eq. (
3.36
) to Roothaan equation Eq. (
3.76
).
First ignore the dependency on
\mathbf{R}
.

(1)
From
E[\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}]
Eq. (
3.36
), by taking the variation, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\updelta}{\updelta(\phi_{j})^{*}}E[\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}]=2\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\phi_{j},

(3.92)

as in Eq. (
3.58
).
Leveraging the relation between derivative and variation, we have
\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}E(\mathbf{C}):=\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}E[\{\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\}_{i}]=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\sum_{j}\frac{\updelta}{\updelta(\phi_{j})^{*}}E[\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}](\mathrm{x})\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}^{*}(\mathrm{x})=2\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\sum_{j}\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}(\mathrm{x})\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{j})^{*}\left\langle\alpha^{\prime}\right\rvert(\mathrm{x})=2\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\sum_{j}\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}(\mathrm{x})\delta^{j}_{k}\left\langle\beta\right\rvert(\mathrm{x})=2\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{k}}(\mathrm{x})\left\langle\beta\right\rvert(\mathrm{x})=2\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{k}\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rangle(\mathrm{x})\left\langle\beta\right\rvert(\mathrm{x})=2\sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{k}\langle\beta|\hat{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}|\alpha\rangle=2(\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{C})\mathbf{C})^{\beta}_{k}
.
By Eq. (
0.3
) and Eq. (
3.81
), we have:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}E(\mathbf{C})=2\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{C})\mathbf{C}=2(\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{P})-\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{P}))\mathbf{C}.

(3.93)

For the orthonormality constraint, when adopting the general complex derivative Eq. (
0.2
), we have

\frac{\updelta}{\updelta(\phi_{l})^{*}}\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}(\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle-\delta^{i}_{j})=\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}(\delta^{i}_{l}\phi_{j}+\delta_{lj}\phi_{i^{\prime}}\delta^{i^{\prime}i})=\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{l}\phi_{j}+\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{i^{\prime}}_{l}\phi_{i^{\prime}}
.
So
\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}\left\lparen\left\langle\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\middle|\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}\right\rangle-\delta^{i}_{j}\right\rparen=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\sum_{l}\frac{\updelta}{\updelta(\phi_{l})^{*}}\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle(\mathrm{x})\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{l}}^{*}(\mathrm{x})=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,\sum_{l}\frac{\updelta}{\updelta(\phi_{l})^{*}}\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}\left\langle\phi_{i}\middle|\phi_{j}\right\rangle(\mathrm{x})\delta^{l}_{k}\left\langle\beta\right\rvert(\mathbf{x})=\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\,(\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{k}\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}(\mathrm{x})+\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{i}_{k}\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\mathrm{x}))\left\langle\beta\right\rvert(\mathbf{x})=\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{k}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{j}\left\langle\beta\middle|\alpha\right\rangle+\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{i}_{k}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\left\langle\beta\middle|\alpha\right\rangle
, or:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}\left\langle\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\middle|\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}\right\rangle=2\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}\bm{\mathcal{E}}.

(3.94)

(2)
From
E[\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}]
Eq. (
3.36
), by plugging in the basis expansion,
we have
E(\mathbf{C}):=E[\{\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\}_{i}]=E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C}))
which recovers Eq. (
3.83
), where
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C}):=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}
.
Then
\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}E(\mathbf{C})=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{(\mathbf{P}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha})^{*}}E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C}))\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}(\mathbf{P}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{C}))^{*}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C})))^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\partial_{(\mathbf{C}^{\beta}_{k})^{*}}\sum_{i}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i})^{*}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C})))^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\sum_{i}(\delta^{\beta\alpha}\delta_{ki}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\delta^{ii^{\prime\prime}}+\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\delta^{i}_{k})=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C})))^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}(\delta^{\beta\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}_{k}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}+\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{k}\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha^{\prime}})=(\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C})))^{\beta}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}_{k}+(\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C})))^{\beta}_{\alpha}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{k}=2(\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{C}))\mathbf{C})^{\beta}_{k}
.
Using Eq. (
3.86
), we have:

\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}E(\mathbf{C})=2(\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{P})-\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{P}))\mathbf{C}
.

For the orthonormality constraint
\left\langle\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\middle|\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}\right\rangle=\delta^{i}_{j}
, plugging in the basis expansion yields
\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{I}
.
Introducing Lagrange multipliers
\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}
, and noting
\mathbf{S}
is Hermitian, we have

\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}\tr(\bm{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{I}))\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}2\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}\bm{\mathcal{E}}
,
or
\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{j}_{i}\left\langle\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\middle|\phi_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}\right\rangle=2\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}\bm{\mathcal{E}}
.

Results of the two processes coincide. Also note that
\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E(\mathbf{P})=\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{C})
.
The orbital solution satisfies:

\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{C}^{\star})\mathbf{C}^{\star}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}^{\star}\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{\star}
and
{\mathbf{C}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}^{\star}=\mathbf{I}
,
which takes the form of the Roothaan equation Eq. (
3.76
).

When using SCF (self-consistent field),
i.e.
, iteratively solving generalized eigenvalue problems, orbital orthogonality can naturally be achieved if there is no degeneracy,
as discussed in Sec.
3.4
(Paragraph “Orbital orthonormality”) and Sec.
3.3
(beginning).
Hence only normalization constraint is required and the Lagrange multiplier matrix is diagonal,
\bm{\upvarepsilon}=\Diag[\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{N}]
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{C}^{\star})\mathbf{C}^{\star}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}^{\star}\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star},\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{C}^{\star}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}^{\star}=\mathbf{I},

(3.95)

which is the Roothaan equation Eq. (
3.76
).

3.6
Atomic Force Calculation

Using SCF orbital:

\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+\tr\Big(\big(\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})\big)^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+\tr\Big(2\big(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+2\tr\Big(\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)
.
From the constraint
{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{I}
, we have

\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}+{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}+{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}=0
,
so
2{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}=-{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}
, and:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})={}

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})-\tr\Big(\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})-\tr\Big(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)

(3.96)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})-\tr\Big(\mathbf{Q}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big),

(3.97)

where
\mathbf{Q}:=\mathbf{C}\bm{\upvarepsilon}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger},\mathbf{Q}^{\alpha}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\sum_{i}\bm{\upvarepsilon}_{i}\mathbf{C}^{\alpha}_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i})^{*}

is the energy-weighted density matrix
[
15
]
.

Using density matrix gives the same result:

\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+\tr\Big(\big(\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})\big)^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+\tr\Big(\big(\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}+\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)
.
From the constraint
\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}
, we have
\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}=\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\big(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)
, so:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})={}

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})-\tr\big(\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})-\tr\big(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\big).

(3.98)

Noting that
\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{C}^{\star}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}}^{\dagger}
, from Eqs. (
3.89
,
3.95
)
, we have

\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star})\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{f}^{\textnormal{HF}}(\mathbf{P}^{\star})\mathbf{C}^{\star}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}^{\star}\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}}^{\dagger}
.
Since
\mathbf{S}
is non-singular, this implies:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{\star}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{\star}=\mathbf{C}^{\star}\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}{\mathbf{C}^{\star}}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{Q}^{\star}.

(3.99)

This then equates Eq. (
3.98
) to Eq. (
3.97
).

But from derivation using SCF orbital,

\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+\tr\Big(2\big(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+\tr\Big(\nabla_{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\tr\big(\big({\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{I}\big)\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\Big)=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})+\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\tr\big(\big({\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{I}\big)\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)
,
and
\diag\big(\big({\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{I}\big)\bm{\upvarepsilon}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\big)=0
since columns of
\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}
are exactly
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}
-normalized for any
\mathbf{R}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})=(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}).

(3.100)

Similarly, from the derivation using density matrix, we can obtain the same conclusion Eq. (
3.100
) above, since the idempotency
\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}
holds exactly since
{\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{I}
exactly since the
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}
-orthogonality also holds exactly in general since
\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}
are eigenvectors of different eigenvalues in general.
This Eq. (
3.100
) is also noted and acknowledged in the original paper of Pulay force
[
16
]
(p.199, first equation in Sec. 2, for which he cited Hellmann’s
[
7
]
and Feynman’s work
[
3
]
).

However,
(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{C}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})
and
(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})
are not the Hellmann-Feynman force (Sec.
4.3.1
).
Noting that
E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{C}):=E_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger})
, in Eq. (
3.83
) or equivalently Eq. (
3.84
), only a part of
{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne},\mathbf{R}}
is the Hellmann-Feynman force:

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})={}

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\bar{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{R}}+\frac{1}{2}{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}(\bar{\bm{\mathsf{J}}}_{\mathbf{R}}-\bar{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}_{\mathbf{R}})\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}

(3.101)

\displaystyle{}+\underbrace{\underbrace{{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\big(\langle\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R}}|\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}V_{\textnormal{Ne},\mathbf{R}}|\alpha_{\mathbf{R}}\rangle\big)^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}}_{\text{Hellmann-Feynman force}}+{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\big(\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R}}|V_{\textnormal{Ne},\mathbf{R}}|\alpha_{\mathbf{R}}\rangle+\langle\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R}}|V_{\textnormal{Ne},\mathbf{R}}|\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\alpha_{\mathbf{R}}\rangle\big)^{(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha)}}_{{\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}}}^{\dagger}\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\bar{\mathbf{V}}_{\textnormal{Ne},\mathbf{R}}}.

(3.102)

The rest part of
(\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}E_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{P}^{\star}_{\mathbf{R}})
is called the wavefunction force in
[
16
]
.
In principle, the Hellmann-Feynman theorem can be applied and the Hellmann-Feynman force can well approximate the true force.
But under atomic basis which is incomplete, the condition that the Hellmann-Feynman theorem holds (true ground-state wavefunction) does not hold exactly, causing error in the force.
The error can be corrected by other terms in Eq. (
3.102
), which appear due to the dependency of the basis functions on atom coordinates.

3.7
Correlation Missed by the Hartree-Fock Method

Define the
correlation energy

E^{\textnormal{HF}}_{\textnormal{C}}
as the difference between the true ground-state energy and the minimal HF energy.
It reflects the approximation gap due to the Slater-determinant form (the HF form) of wavefunction.
Since the Slater determinant is the wavefunction of non-interacting systems, this gap then arises from the effect of interaction, hence called “correlation”.

From
[
11
]
,
“Electron correlation is mainly caused by the
dynamical correlation
(Sinanoğlu, 1964, according to
[
19
]
),
i.e.
, the instantaneous repulsion of the electrons, which is not covered by the effective HF potential. Pictorially speaking, the electrons get often too close to each other in the Hartree-Fock scheme, because
the electrostatic interaction is treated in only an average manner
.”
“The second main contribution to
E^{\textnormal{HF}}_{\textnormal{C}}
is the
non-dynamical
or
static correlation
. It is related to the fact that in certain circumstances the ground-state Slater determinant is not a good approximation to the true ground-state, because there are other Slater determinants with comparable energies. A typical example is provided by one of the famous laboratories of quantum chemistry, the
\mathrm{H}_{2}
molecule.”

RHF deviates from the experimental truth as the inter-H distance increases, where dynamical correlation becomes a minor issue (since the Coulomb interaction is weak).
When choosing the one-electron orbitals as
\{\phi_{\textnormal{g}}(\vec{r})\delta^{\uparrow}_{s},\phi_{\textnormal{g}}(\vec{r})\delta^{\downarrow}_{s}\}

where the molecular orbital
\phi_{\textnormal{g}}(\vec{r}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\phi_{\textnormal{L}}(\vec{r})+\phi_{\textnormal{R}}(\vec{r}))
is composed of atomic orbitals of the left-H and right-H,
the Slater determinant gives equal weights to
\{\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}\mathrm{H}^{\downarrow},\mathrm{H}^{\downarrow}\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow},\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\mathrm{H},\mathrm{H}^{\downarrow\uparrow}\mathrm{H}\}

(Eq. (
3.20
) with
N^{\uparrow}=N^{\downarrow}=1
gives the same value when
(s_{1},s_{2})
is
(\uparrow,\downarrow)
or
(\downarrow,\uparrow)
, and
\phi_{\textnormal{g}}
assigns a spin value to either atoms with equal weights),
while the latter two states are less likely for distant H atoms.
From another perspective, another basis for the atomic orbitals combination
\phi_{\textnormal{u}}(\vec{r}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\phi_{\textnormal{L}}(\vec{r})-\phi_{\textnormal{R}}(\vec{r}))
should be considered,
and the dominant states
\{\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}\mathrm{H}^{\downarrow},\mathrm{H}^{\downarrow}\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}\}
for distant H atoms is then representable as the summation of the Slater determinants using both molecular orbitals.

UHF ameliorates this problem a lot, since it allows the two dominant states
\{\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}\mathrm{H}^{\downarrow},\mathrm{H}^{\downarrow}\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}\}
where the electrons are unpaired.
But its accuracy also suffers from not being the total-spin eigenfunction.

4
Density Functional Theory

4.1
The Hohenberg-Kohn Theorems

The two HK theorems (1964)
[
8
]
follow the variational principle of the Schrödinger equation.
They laid the foundation of DFT that it makes physical sense to take the ground-state density as the basic variable.
Define the system-independent operator as:

\displaystyle\hat{F}:=\hat{T}+\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}.

(4.1)

The first theorem:

The non-degenerate ground-state density
\rho_{0}
of a system uniquely determines the external potential
V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho_{0}]}(\vec{r})
(up to a constant) of the system,
consequently the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{[\rho_{0}]}=\hat{T}+\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho_{0}]}
(
c.f.
, Eq. (
2.87
)) and all of the entire the system.

(
V_{\textnormal{ext}}(\vec{r})
is restricted to a local, one-electron potential s.t.
\langle\psi|\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ext}}|\psi\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi]}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}}]
.)

Otherwise, there is a different (more than a constant)
V^{\prime}_{\textnormal{ext}}
whose ground-state
\psi^{\prime}_{0}
yields the same density
\rho_{[\psi^{\prime}_{0}]}=\rho_{[\psi_{0}]}
.
By the variational principle,
E_{0}=\langle\psi_{0}|\hat{F}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ext}}|\psi_{0}\rangle<\langle\psi^{\prime}_{0}|\hat{F}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ext}}|\psi^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
, while
E^{\prime}_{0}=\langle\psi^{\prime}_{0}|\hat{F}+\hat{V}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{ext}}|\psi^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
.
Subtracting them yields
E_{0}-E^{\prime}_{0}<\langle\psi^{\prime}_{0}|\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ext}}-\hat{V}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{ext}}|\psi^{\prime}_{0}\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi^{\prime}_{0}]}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}}-V^{\prime}_{\textnormal{ext}}]
.
Symmetrically,
E^{\prime}_{0}-E_{0}<\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi_{0}]}}[V^{\prime}_{\textnormal{ext}}-V_{\textnormal{ext}}]
.
Adding the two inequalities yields a contradiction
0<\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi^{\prime}_{0}]}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}}-V^{\prime}_{\textnormal{ext}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi_{0}]}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}}-V^{\prime}_{\textnormal{ext}}]=0
.

This theorem indicates the ground-state wavefunction
\psi_{[\rho_{0}]}
of a system is a functional of the ground-state density
\rho_{0}
of the system,
hence the ground-state energy:
\exists\text{ functional }E_{0}[\rho_{0}]
.
Since
E_{0}[\rho_{0}]=\langle\psi_{[\rho_{0}]}|\hat{F}|\psi_{[\rho_{0}]}\rangle+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho_{0}]}]

where the second term is an explicit functional of
\rho_{0}
if we know the
V_{\textnormal{ext}}
that yields
\rho_{0}
,
we know
if
\rho_{0}
is the ground-state density of some system, then the “kinetic + ee” energy of that system is uniquely determined by
\rho_{0}
.
This defines the
Hohenberg-Kohn functional

F^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho_{0}]
.

By now, the
\rho
is still required to be the ground-state density of
some
system (
i.e.

\exists V_{\textnormal{ext}}
s.t. this
\rho
is the ground-state density).
Characterizing such
\rho
is called the
V
ext
-representability
problem (
i.e.
, finding the pre-image set of
V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho]}
; Lieb’s
\mathscr{A}_{N}
set).
A commonly satisfactory, much
weaker
condition is
N-representability
, meaning that
\rho
stems from an antisymmetric wavefunction of
N
electrons (Lieb’s
\mathscr{I}_{N}
set; for the Levy-Lieb functional).

The second theorem:

The ground-state energy
E[V_{\textnormal{ext}}]
and density
\rho_{[V_{\textnormal{ext}}]}
of a system specified by
V_{\textnormal{ext}}(\vec{r})
are:

\displaystyle(E[V_{\textnormal{ext}}],\rho_{[V_{\textnormal{ext}}]})=\pminargmin_{\rho:\text{V\textsubscript{ext}-repr}}\big\{E[\rho]:=F^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[V_{\textnormal{ext}}]\big\}.

(4.2)

The theorem follows the variational principle.
Note that it is inappropriate to minimize
E_{0}[\rho]=F^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho]}]
, in which
V_{\textnormal{ext}}
also changes with
\rho
so the minimization process is not for a fixed system.

The Levy constrained-search approach (1979)
[
13
]
.

The variational principle can be carried out in two stages:

\displaystyle E[V_{\textnormal{ext}}]=\min_{\psi}\big\langle\psi\big|\hat{F}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ext}}\big|\psi\big\rangle=\min_{\rho:\text{N-repr}}\min_{\psi:\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}\big\langle\psi\big|\hat{F}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ext}}\big|\psi\big\rangle=\min_{\rho:\text{N-repr}}\Big\lparen\underbrace{\min_{\psi:\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}\big\langle\psi\big|\hat{F}\big|\psi\big\rangle}_{=:F[\rho],\textbf{ universal functional}}+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[V_{\textnormal{ext}}]\Big\rparen.

(4.3)

The
universal functional

F[\rho]=(T+E_{\textnormal{ee}})[\rho]
(Lieb’s
F^{\textnormal{Lv}}[\rho]
) (allows all N-representable
\rho
; Lieb’s
\mathscr{I}_{N}
) differs from
F^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]
(allows only V
ext
-representable
\rho
; Lieb’s
\mathscr{A}_{N}
) only in the domains.
They coincide for V
ext
-representable
\rho
.
This formulation recovers the two HK theorems, and also lifts the non-degenerate restriction.

Corollaries

(1)
For a non-interacting system described by Eq. (
3.16
) whose solutions are Slater determinants (see the Explanation of A2 in Sec.
3.1.1
), there is no
\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}
term.
So the universal/HK functional in this case is
a functional of density representing the kinetic energy of a non-interacting system, denoted as
T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]
, although its explicit form in
\rho
is unknown.
When the non-interacting system is constructed to recover the same density as an (strictly) interacting system,
T_{\textnormal{S}}<T

[
11
]
.

(2)
Note that from Eq. (
2.89
),
F[\rho]=\min_{P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}:\rho_{[P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}]}=\rho}T[P^{1}_{1^{\prime}}]+E_{\textnormal{ee}}[\rho_{12}]=\min_{P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}:\rho_{[P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}]}=\rho}T[P^{1}_{1^{\prime}}]+E_{\textnormal{XC}}[\rho;h_{\textnormal{XC}}]+J[\rho]
.
Since
J[\rho]
is naturally a functional of
\rho
thus fixed in the minimization,

(T+E_{\textnormal{XC}})[\rho]:=\min_{P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}:\rho_{[P^{12}_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}]}=\rho}T[P^{1}_{1^{\prime}}]+E_{\textnormal{XC}}[\rho;h_{\textnormal{XC}}]
is also a density functional.

Some details.

There are two ways to define density functionals (see Lieb’s paper
[
14
]
or Sec.
4.4
for more rigorous details):

(1)

F^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]:=F[\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}]
,
T^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]:=T[\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}]
(this is not Lieb’s
T_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{KS}}
; this considers interaction),
where
\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}
satisfies
\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}\in\argmin_{\psi:\text{N-repr}}F[\psi]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi]}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho]}]
and
\rho_{[\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}]}=\rho
.
By construction, they are defined only for
\rho
that is some potential’s ground-state density (Lieb’s
\mathscr{A}_{N}
).

(2)

F[\rho]:=\min_{\psi:\text{N-repr},\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}F[\psi]
,
T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]:=\min_{\psi:\text{N-repr},\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}T[\psi]
(Lieb’s
F^{\textnormal{Lv}}[\rho]
and
T_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{Lv}}[\rho]
).
Denote the minimizers as
\psi_{[\rho]}^{(F)}
and
\psi_{[\rho]}^{(T)}
.
By construction, they can be defined for all N-representable
\rho
(Lieb’s
\mathscr{I}_{N}
), which contains
\mathscr{A}_{N}
.

Now consider their coincidence and their eligibility for finding
E_{0}
for a given
V_{\textnormal{ext}}
.

Obviously, for
\rho\in\mathscr{A}_{N}
,
F[\rho]\leqslant F^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]
since
F[\rho]
is the minimal achievable value by
\rho
.
If equality does not hold, then
F[\psi_{[\rho]}^{(F)}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi_{[\rho]}^{(F)}]}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho]}]=F[\psi_{[\rho]}^{(F)}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho]}]<F[\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho]}]=F[\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}]}}[V_{\textnormal{ext}[\rho]}]

which violates that
\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}
is a minimizer.
So
F[\rho]=F^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]
on
\mathscr{A}_{N}
, and
\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}\in\argmin_{\psi:\text{N-repr},\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}F[\psi]
.
The eligibility is obvious by considering
F[\rho]
in Levy’s two-stage formulation, Eq. (
4.3
).

Obviously,
T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]=T[\psi_{[\rho]}^{(T)}]\leqslant T[\psi_{[\rho]}^{(F)}]=T[\psi_{[\rho]}^{\textnormal{HK}}]=T^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]
, and “
=
” holds only when
W_{\textnormal{ee}}=\mathrm{const}
(non-interacting case).
As for eligibility, in a general (interacting) case,
T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]+E_{\textnormal{ee}}[\rho]:=\min_{\psi:\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}T[\psi]+\min_{\psi:\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}E_{\textnormal{ee}}[\psi]<\min_{\psi:\rho_{[\psi]}=\rho}T[\psi]+E_{\textnormal{ee}}[\psi]=F[\rho]
, so using
T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]+E_{\textnormal{ee}}[\rho]
is not eligible.

The results also hold when replacing
E_{\textnormal{ee}}
with
E_{\textnormal{XC}}
, since the difference
J[\rho]
is an explicit functional of
\rho
.

Does
T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho_{[\psi_{[V]}^{\textnormal{KS}}]}]=T[\psi_{[V]}^{\textnormal{KS}}]
where
\psi_{[V]}^{\textnormal{KS}}:=\argmin_{\psi_{\textnormal{S}}}T[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]+J[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]+E_{\overline{\textnormal{XC}}}[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]}}[V]
?
Obviously l.h.s
\leqslant
r.h.s by definition of
T_{\textnormal{S}}
.
Also, the additional terms
J
,
E_{\overline{\textnormal{XC}}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[V]
are all functionals of
\rho
(for
E_{\overline{\textnormal{XC}}}
, it is defined as
(T+E_{\textnormal{XC}})[\rho]-T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]
),
so following a similar argument as for
F^{\textnormal{HK}}=F
above,
\psi_{[V]}^{\textnormal{KS}}
also minimizes
T[\psi]
for the given density
\rho_{[\psi_{[V]}^{\textnormal{KS}}]}
.
So the kinetic energy computed from the DFT orbital solution can be taken as the label for learning
T_{\textnormal{S}}
.
More generally, this conclusion also holds even when the specifically chosen approximation
E_{\overline{\textnormal{XC}}}
is not exact as long as it is a density functional (the inexactness only biases the
\rho
solution from fitting the given system).

4.2
The Kohn-Sham Approach

Background

The Thomas-Fermi model (1927)
[
18
,
2
]
and extensions “fail miserably when results better than mere qualitative trends are the target”
[
11
]
.
The scheme approximates the kinetic energy by that of the uniform electron gas,

12
12

12

Why regarded “uniform” while
\rho(\vec{r})
changes spacially? Because it is not regarding the density as uniform, but taking the energy density at
\vec{r}
as the energy density as if the density is everywhere equal to the value
\rho(\vec{r})
.

\displaystyle T^{\textnormal{TF}}[\rho]:=\frac{3}{10}(3\pi^{2})^{\frac{2}{3}}\int\mathrm{d}\vec{r}\,\rho^{\frac{5}{3}}(\vec{r}).

(4.4)

This approximation does not meet chemical interest which is on the bonds arising from non-uniform electron density.
Calls for more accurate kinetic energy estimation.
The Kohn-Sham approach
[
12
]
is developed in 1965.

Overview

“Introduce a fictitious,
non-interacting
reference system such that the major part of the kinetic energy can be computed to good accuracy.
The remainder is merged with the non-classical contributions
E_{\textnormal{XC}}
to the electron-electron repulsion, which are also unknown but usually fairly small.
By this method, as much information as possible is computed exactly, leaving only a small part of the total energy to be determined by an approximate functional.”
[
11
]

Description

Finding the ground-state energy of a given system with potential
V_{\textnormal{ext}}
can be done by varying density to minimize Eq. (
4.2
) or Eq. (
4.3
), but the problem is that
F^{\textnormal{HK}}
or
F
, or equivalently
(T+E_{\textnormal{XC}})[\rho]
, is unknown.
But we know the exact wavefunction of a non-interacting system
\hat{H}=\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}
(Eq. (
3.16
)) is in the form of the Slater determinant
\psi_{\textnormal{S}}
(see the Explanation of A2 in Sec.
3.1.1
).
Choose the effective one-electron potential
V_{\textnormal{S}}
such that the ground-state density of the reference system
\rho_{\textnormal{S}}(\vec{r})=\sum_{k\in[N]}\sum_{s}\left\lvert\phi_{k}^{s}(\vec{r})\right\rvert^{2}
(Eq. (
3.10
)) equals that
\rho_{0}(\vec{r})
of the real system of interacting electrons,
\hat{H}=\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{Ne}}+\hat{W}_{\textnormal{ee}}
(Eq. (
2.87
)).
If the energy functionals of the two systems coincide, the real ground-state energy can also be recovered by the reference system.
(The significance of the HK theorems here is that it tells us it suffices to use a reference system that shares the energy as a functional only of the density,
but not have to be of the wavefunction which is definitely not possible (the real one is interacting while the reference’s is not; particularly the real one has correlation for antiparallel-spin electrons while the reference’s does not; see Eq. (
3.12
)).)

To achieve this, reformulate the
real, target
total energy Eq. (
2.89
) by leveraging Corollaries (1) and (2) that both
T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]
and
(T+E_{\textnormal{XC}})[\rho]
are functionals of density
\rho
:

\displaystyle E[\rho]=\overbrace{(T+E_{\textnormal{XC}})[\rho]+\overbrace{E_{\textnormal{Ne}}[\rho]}^{E_{\textnormal{ext}}^{\lambda=1}[\rho]}+J[\rho]}^{\text{interacting, real, physical view}}=\overbrace{T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]+\overbrace{E_{\textnormal{Ne}}[\rho]+J[\rho]+\underbrace{(T+E_{\textnormal{XC}})[\rho]-T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]}_{=:E_{\overline{\textnormal{XC}}}[\rho]}}^{E_{\textnormal{ext},\textnormal{S}}[\rho]=E_{\textnormal{ext}}^{\lambda=0}[\rho]}}^{\text{non-interacting, reference, effective view}}.

(4.5)

This
E_{\overline{\textnormal{XC}}}[\rho]
overloads the name “exchange-correlation energy” in the KS scheme.
It contains not only the non-classical effects of self-interaction correction, exchange and correlation (
i.e.
,
E_{\textnormal{XC}}
), but also a portion belonging to the kinetic energy (roughly
T^{\textnormal{HK}}[\rho]-T_{\textnormal{S}}[\rho]
).
By construction, it is a functional of electron density
\rho
, unlike
E_{\textnormal{XC}}
in the general case,
so taking its variation w.r.t orbitals gives a
local
effective potential, which is what needed for a non-interacting reference system.
This enables finding the ground-state energy by varying orbitals:

\displaystyle\min_{\rho:\text{N-repr}}E[\rho]\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}{}

\displaystyle\min_{\rho:\text{N-repr}}\Big\lparen\min_{\psi_{\textnormal{S}}:\rho_{[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]}=\rho}T[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]+E_{\textnormal{ext},\textnormal{S}}[\rho]\Big\rparen=\min_{\rho:\text{N-repr}}\Big\lparen\min_{\psi_{\textnormal{S}}:\rho_{[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]}=\rho}T[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]+E_{\textnormal{ext},\textnormal{S}}[\rho_{[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]}]\Big\rparen

(4.6)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\min_{\psi_{\textnormal{S}}:\text{N-repr}}T[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]+E_{\textnormal{ext},\textnormal{S}}[\rho_{[\psi_{\textnormal{S}}]}],

(4.7)

and similarly to Eq. (
3.58
), taking the variation of the objective w.r.t orbital
\phi_{i}
subject to the normalization constraint gives:

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\textnormal{KS}}\phi_{i}:=\big(\hat{T}+\hat{V}_{\textnormal{S}}^{\textnormal{KS}}\big)\phi_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\phi_{i},\quad\text{where}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces
